ALL AfEN ARE LIARS

S 3 writes a Powlar Author.

Not so bad as that I hope, although I must admit some men are wicked liars ; in fact, it's only by accident they ever speak the truth. It is not a lie when Gershon, the Universal Furnithing Contractor, 44, Oldham-street ; 17, 19, and 21, Hilton-street. Manchester, states that he never attempts to put before the puolic common new London made rubbish, more like orange boxes, for high-class second-hand ; it is not a lie when Gershon states that he never attempts to trespass upon his neighbour's business. Why should ke ? It only causes a bad feeling. Gershon's motto is live and let live. He sticks to the one thing he understands, namely, furnishing. If you can't furnish with a man that understands his business, you cannot do so with men that don't. Gershon has for years cautioned the pubic against buying common new London furniture for second hand, and he still does so. However distasteful it may be to others, Gershon still takes his stand upon the rock of " Honesty and Justice," which are the guiding principles of his business, and from which precents time nor tide will muse him to recede. BErrmt THAN A SOFT ANSWER.—The hotel clerk can always be depended upon to retain his self- possession under the most trying circumstances. It is related that a certain lady in the dramatic profession, who is always trying to appear young, albeit she is "mote than seven, and looks it, was on the road last year with a travelling comedy company. The troupe got into town late one Sun- day evening and on reaching a hotel she was shown up five flight of stairs to an apartment numbered 20, almost under the tiles. Enraged, she flew down and shrieked at the young man behind the counter: "I am placed in No. 20 ! " "Yes," was the answer; "we give every lady an apartment, if possible, corresponding with her age. Mrs. —, the old woman of your company, is in 62, and Miss --, the juvenile actress is in 44, and that is why I put you in 20." The lady smiled serenely-, thanked the attendant most sweetly, and, without a murmur, returned to the garret. "CAPITAL" CHAT.

A report reaches me that President Kruger will shortly tender his resignation. He has taken the Transvaal through a great crisis, and he feels that he may now rest from his labours, leaving the immediate future in the hands of younger men,who can depart from his policy if they please. Some- thing of the kind is coming, but it will not come until events in Rhodesia are quieter.

An important statement on the Eastern crisis was made on Monday night by Mr. Lowles, M.P. Addressing a meeting in t;horeditch 'town Hall on Armenian affairs, he said that as a humble member of Parliament be had felt it his duty to call at the Foreign Office and ascertain what steps were being taken to put a atop to the massacres. He was authorised to say that we were confronted with the agreement of three Great Powers that if England single-handed attacked Turkey, on what- ever excuse or pretext, she should be face to face with that combination. If England flied a single shot or took action alone, it would mean at this moment European war. He simply stated that solid fact, and they would guess from what he had said before, the source from:which his intormation came. The statement was received by the meet- ing with some commotion.

The London Conservative members have still to face their constituents on the Rating Bill. Mr. Massey-Mainwaring, tackled by some members of the Clerkenwell Vestry, says he will enter into ex- planations at a public meeting. This is precisely what is wanted. Lively times are in prospect for skirmishers and chuckers-out. Some of the con- stituencies are altogether dissatisfied with the votes of their members on other matters, notably on the Education Bill and the water question. Some M.P.'s feel considerably uneasy in consequence, and public meetings are just the danger they intend to avoid as long as possible. But their wisdom will hardly be justified by events, and they had better get a disagreeable business over before it gets worse. _ .

The Irish Nationalists repudiate with some warmth the statement that a new dynamite-using association has been formed in London, affiliated to some league of the kind in New York. There are some curious samples of human beings in London, but the mad Nationalist is not one of them, so far as the use of explosives is concerned. The notion of respectable professional men count4naucing attacks of this kind is preposterous. They are interested in the political movement more closely than many persons believe, but they are simply politicians, and they bave no desire to be anything more. The police may be misled by reports in the matter, as they were in May last about a big dinner of desperadoes, including some M.P.'s. Bit it is just as likely that some foolish scribe has been mis- let by a waggish detective.

If a by-election were to take place just now it is doubtful whether the Irish Unionist Alliance would send a single worker into the constituency. It is not that funds are lacking, for the money colected or guaranteed prior to the general election is not, yet exhausted, and, in addition to this, substantial contributions have since been received. But the Irish landlords, who are the backbone of the or- ganisation, are incensed at the manner in which they were "betrayed" last session, and they would not be sorry to have an opportunity of displaying their resentment. Some of them, indeed, go so far as to advocate the entire closing of the London office, which during the last few year has been in constant touch with the Tory headquarters for electioneering purposes. The Tory wirmpullers are perfectly cognisant of the turn affairs have taken, but their equanimity is in no way disturbed. As a matter of fact, the methods of the Irish clntingent were never to their taste, for where the volunteers waxed energetic and enthusiastic the regulars could not for very shame remain idle.

Names for streets in London are chosen some- what erratically, more very often with the object of immortalising a jerry builder than of perpetuating historic associations. At Tuesday's meeting of the Chelsea Vestry, the chairman made a protest against this system, and suggested a list. of notable Chelsea people from which street names may be selected. Why, he asks, adopt sueh abominable names as Raasay, Bifron, Meek, and Vicat,when Sir Thomas Moore, a resident of Chelsea, has been passed over? He instances the present bad policy by the removal of an appropriate name for that of Fernshaw-road, although there has not been ferns or shaws in Chelsea for ages.

The meaning of Mr. Joseph Arch's announcement that he does not intend to resign his seat in Parlia- ment because an annuity is being got up for him is somewhat obscure at first. Fortunately I can throw light upon it. The local Conservatives in Norfolk would like to get him out of the way, and as soon as they heard that the annuity proposal was being made they laid stress on some of his votes on the flating Bill, upon his advancing years, and began a clamour for his resignation. Mr. Arch has given his answer to this foolish move. He intends to re- main what he is—a steady-going, careful voting,and very useful member of Parliament. He speaks less than formerly, but his views have undergone no change, and his supporters in Norfolk are just as proud of him as ever.

A writer in the "Woman at Home" nens the praises of Lady Harcourt. Sir William Harcourt, she Rays, is happy in the collaboration of a wife ideal in the circumstances. She hints that Sir William is the kind of man peculiarly in need of the gentle influence of a graceful wife. There is in his ordinary manner and address no medium, be- tween extreme urbanity and—something diametri- cally opposite to urbanity. But since he married, en secondes noces, Mrs. Ives, she has rained sweet influences over the household, and Sir William has become quite urbane as a host. Lady Harcourt is seen at her best as hostess at her country home in the New Forest. She does not set uu as a brilliant talker, nor lay herself out to be a leader of fashion in dress or other social matters. She is just a woman, but one of innate goad nature, kindly feel- ing.high intelligencemd perfect breeding. Though, as far as her friends know, she never meddles in literature, she inherits from her father—the histor- ion of the Netherlands—a keen literary taste. She has read most books worth reading, end is at home with those who write hooks, even if some of the products are not of the best.

The police authorities here are not now Vito so sure as they were that they will obtain from France the extradition of Tynan. In fact there are reasons for thinking that the French Government will not grant it, and his appeal to the United States Am- bassador goes far to confirm the belief. Whether ultimately he is extradited or riot, that appeal will delay the proceedings. His assertion that he is a naturalised citizen of the United States will compel the!intervention of the elovernment of the American Republic on his behalf. The police, in their appli- cation for his extradition, have taken their stand upon the wrong foot, for if they had demanded his extradition on the izrotincl of his implication in a dynamite conspiracy they would assuredly have secured their man without any difficulty. But as regards Tynan they may not have been armed with the necassary proof of such implication, whereas they reckoned on having a strong case against him on the count of the Pho3nix Park murders. It is fortunate that Tynan has raised the question of his nationality before being handed over to this coun- try, for be is not worth another quarrel or misun- derstanding with the United States.

It is notorious that the official Turk is not very particular about the means he adopt for raising revenue, and the following story, which T have from a person who was cognisant of all the circum- stances, serves to illustrate the methods whereby the exchequer is replenished. In a certain Syrian town A owned a house with a fine" view." B bcught a piece of ground just in view of A's property, with the object of building a house for himself thereon. But the erection of B's house would have obstructed A's view, so A promptly laid the matter before sympathetic "authorities "at Constantinople. The latter informed A that although B had purchased the plot of ground he had not bought the air above it, and that A could even yet preserve bin" view" by purchasing this air him- self. Of course this suggestion was acted on, with the result that B was forbidden to erect his con- templated house because the building would en- croach on " air " that was the property of A. So the ground which had been purchased es a desir- able building site was rendered useless for building purposes, and B could only turn it to account as a garden. I need hardly add that A paid a substan- tial consideration for the " air" on B's land. And this was in Syria, where the administration is understood to be more of the Turkish Empire.just than in most other parts

LADIES' LETTER.

MY DEAR NELLIE,

In the way of new millinery there is nothing very startling to record. One pretty hat I have seen was a "Duchess of Marlborough' hat, with a high-peaked crown, trimmed with twists and rosettes of three different colours. The shape is of black fancy straw, and a handsome tuft of black and white ostrich feathers appears at the left side. The top twist of ribbon is of rose-pink moire, the middle of cream moire, and the bottom one of black moire. On the right, coming from under the brim, is a twisted black moire ribbon, which fastens up on the left shoulder with a bunch of pink roses and buds.

A smart cycling hat is of coarse wheat-coloured natural straw, trimmed with line grass lawn ribbons, hieli look like transparent gauze, with a border of blue satin at either edge. There are quills at the Ift side, and under the brim at Ole back are blue fl9ivers to match the satin border of the ribbon. This charming hat can be made in any colour. as these pretty ribbons, which go perfectly with grass lawn costumes, can be had with pink, gteen and other cokured borders, and the Howe, s at the back in each ease match the :atin border.

Already the first touch of autumn is upon us, and it is quite time to be thinking of warmer wraps, and substantial garments al,-,ogether. Cipes will again be in evidence, the smarter ones of velvet, satin, or tine cloth, sumptuously trimmed, and those of the tailor-made type fashioned with severe simplicity from thick cuvei t coating, Harris's tweed, or all-wool frieze.

A becoming cape may be made of dark green satin, with fronts and side panels of cream velvet, closely, but lightly, embroidered with gold, while coiled ostrich tips from the neck ruche and adorn the front.

A very smart and serviceable wrap is made of thick fawn beaver cloth, strapped right round and down each seam, and having a straight, double- breasted front with big horn button, and a cosy cod ir may be made of skunk fur. Nice girls always avoid undue elaboration and all eccentricity of style or material in their garments, and for ordinary wear a good calico, such as the "Niblick" make, not too tine and trimmed with Swiss embroidery and beautiful fancy stitching, is all that can be desired. Garments made in this fashion, plain, but good, wear splendidly, resisting even the wicked wiles of the laundress. Then, for evening and very best wear, there should be dainty garments and fine lmrcloth, trimmed with Valen- ciennes, torchon, or Bedford lace and insertion.

An elaborate costume can be made of fine green canvas, the skirt quite plain, and lined with bright rose sik. which inip,rtA a taint shot effect that is very fascinating. The bodice may have a full vest of white chiffon over white .satin ; while the sleeves, sash, and collar are of shot green and pink merv, with tiny spots of pink. The novel fan drapery at the shoulder is of green satin. Although the tea-gown is a garment that some women wear all the year round, autumn and winter are the scasons in which its comfort and elegance are best appreciated; and now that the country- house time has come round once More, maids and matrons alike are turning their thoughts towards graceful gowns, to be donned when they come in from a long day with the guns or rods. Some new gowns are very beautiful and artistic, and exhibit novel features, while retaining the characteristics of these delightfully comfortable and becoming robes. A most al tractive 'crown is made of velve- teen in a rich, dark shade of green, the back cut ea prineesse; with demi-train, and the front having jaunty bolero bodice pieces, supplemented ,by a s full vest and revers of primrose ondine, edged with oftly-falling frills of fine cream Mauresque lace. The sleeves are remarkably pretty, with just a small shoulder-puff, and close-fitting thence to the elbow, where they terminate with a fanciful cuff of primrose silk and lace. Girls' hats should be very simple in style, like everything else appertaining_ to their toilette. For winter, a velvet Tam o' Shanter, with a velvet bow, or a couple of good quills at the side, is as pretty as anything, and suits most young faces delightfully. The quills, by the way, should be of the best quality procurable; they are cheapest in the end, for common quills look ragged and shabby and in an incredibly short time. Felt hats with low crowns and rather broad brims are also becoming, trimmed with a folded band and nice bow of silk velvet.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY SEPTEMBER 20, 196 TEE SUNDERED HEARTS;

" WOMEN'S CHAT."

The Duke of York is to be invested with the Order of St. Andrew, which is the first decoration in Russia, rank- ing with the Garter, and the other three great Orders of Europe. The Duchess is to receive the Ordeit of the St. Catherine. This is the second great Order in Russia and is for women only of the very highest rank and distinc- tion. It was founded by Peter the Great, as a memorial of the services rendered to him by his wife during his war with the Turks. The Empress is the Grand Mistress, and the members wear a broad pink ribbon with silver border, to which is suspended an image of St. Catherine, and on the left breast is worn a silver star with a white cross on a red ground. The Queen, the Princess of Wales, the Duchess of Coburg, and the Duchess of Con- naught all have the Order of St. Catherine, which was given to Her Majesty by the Emperor Nicholas I. when he visited England in 1844. Many of my readers may not be aware that our Queen was baptised Alexandrina Victoria, the former name being given to her by her father, the Duke of Kent, in compliment to the Emperor of Russia. The Prince Regent, her uncle, subsequently George IV., desired that Georgiana should be the first name of the infant,but the Duke of Kent insisted that it should be Alexandrina. The Prince Regent thereupon declared that the name Georgians could not stand second to any other name whatever, and that therefore the infant must not bear it at all.

In her anxiety to avoid e'ice. Miss Marie Corelli unhappily does not always acleeve that end, and even the pains she takes not to be written about only affords another opportunity of talking about her. The very latest rumoer concerning the gifted novelist is, that she shoots, and rides a bicycle in masculine attire ! As a matter of fact Miss Corelli is even womanly enough to be afraid of guns, and her description of our sex on wheels, would decidedly lead us to suppose that she far from approves of the pastime. " I think." she says, " even men look hideous and undignified on the bike, but women look worse than hideous, they look immodest. The poet's ideal of exquisite womanhood is utterly des- troyed, by the sight ot these perspiring, red faced, lank- haired objects, working their legs treadmill fashion and tearing along every road in the country." We all know, for we have all seen, how much games have influenced girls. The figure of the British girl has almost completely changed in the last decade or so; lawn lennis and cricket seem to have lengthened her arms,and gymnastics her body. She is taller, slighter, more muscular and agile. Constant bicvling, however, will pall her together instead of pulling her out, so say authorities on the subject, and the mus,•les which this exercise calls into play, develops, and promotes, will make for strength rather than beauty. Several of the London streets are just now undergoing repair, and I witnessed an amusing incident the other day, when walking dowr Piccadilly. A gentleman, evi- dently a geologist, was busily engaged in examining and tapping pieces of stone, unearthed by the repairers, and so engrossed was he, in his fascinating pursuit, that he appeared perfectly oblivious of the tact that a large crowd of spectators had gathered round him eagerly watching his every movement. The enthusiast and his audience, had eventually to be" moved on" by the police. Nowadays, the hand-rnade laces our ancestors wore, are so beautifully copied by the machine, that their de- generate descendants accept them gladly, whether it be Italian,or Spanish point, or some of the best handi- work of Ireland. There is however, in the new laces, a disposition to return to Yak or Maltese patterns, especi- ally in piece laces which will be the most used for bodices, and panels. The most delightful light designs ' have been adapted to Chantilly, and Brussels point has been cooled in black and white.

The continued cold and damp has led to a much earlier display of furs than we usually have, and fur waistcoats are the very latest item that has claimed my attention. Built of sealskin they look very smart and comfortable, and allow us to wear a coat and skirt costume much longer than we could hope to do so, without a covering of some description. Seal plush waistcoats are easily made at home, and I would advise those of my readers who are not possessed of sealskin, to indulge in a vest of this particular material, taking care to line the back with a double thickness of flannel. .

It is difficult to assign a cause, but certain it is that muffs are falling into disuse. For one that is carried now there used to be fifty. Is it, I wonder, that women are harder now than they used to be, or is it that warmer gloves are worn. Perhaps though, the cape has assisted in banishing them, and it seems rather prob- able that this is the true solution, as capes are so often furnished with warm pockets in which the fingers may nestle on a cold day. Collectors of Coins, stamps, walking sticks, shoes,fans, Sze.. are by no means rare, but collectors of buttons are distinctly so. The weAlthy eccentric Englishman, who died at Vienna a short time back, left no fewer than 20,000, representing all the uniforms of the different armies in the world. Apropos, the gem of the collec- tion of coins at the British Museum, London, from the point of view of rarity, is a five shilling piece of the Old Pretender, issued under the style of James 111., being the only rine known. There is a curious history attach- ing to it. It belonged to a certain General Yorke-Moore, who took it to the Museum, when Mr. Hawkins was keeper of antiquities, and said he was going to take it out to the Crimea. Mr. Hawkins was so enraged to think that such a risk should be run with so rare a loin, that he threw it back to the General, and it fell upon the floor. General Moore picked it up, and took it with him'as a kind of talisman, to the Crimea. After the General's death it was purchased for the Museum for £250, which is about the largest sum the Bloomsbury authorities have ever given for a single coin. It appears the dairy-farmer is threatened with a novel form of competition, for in Denmark milk can now be bought by the brick! A company has been formed for the regular supply of milk ice, and according to the latest intelligence, contracts have already been made for a weekly delivery f 110,0001bs. of the frozen blocks, in different parts of the world. Those of us who indulge in camphor bags to place among our linen,furs,and gowns will regret to hear that there is a chance of a dearth, as has been the case with whalebone. Scientists and experts are recommending restrictions on the wanton destruction of the trees, and the formation cf plantations. The tree that produces most eamphor is a species of laurel,a relation of the cin- namnn tree, and it attains a vast age. The bulk of the camphor used in Europe comes from Japan and For- mosa.

An excellent method whereby to keep meat and fish fresh is to rub it with powdered hon./. This is an espe- cially good plan when we desire to hang poultry for a few -days. Before cooking them the provisions thus treated should be washed in lukewarm water,when they will be found as sweet and fresh as possible. Borax (it should be Californian) will also preserve milk and cream quite sweetif some powder is sprinkled on the top. The The milk should then be lightly stirred. The quantity to use is a small teaspoonful to each piot. 'CORNFLOUR CAKES.—Take three Ounces of sugar, three ounces of butter or good beef dripping, and beat them lightly together till they resemble cream. Add a lightly beaten egg to the mixture and sufficient vanilla essence to flAvour. Mix and pass through a sieve three ounces of fine flour, threw ounces of cornflour and a tea spooonful of baking powder. Then add to the egg,butter, and sugar. Beat the mixture well together, half fill greased patty pans with the mixture, and bake in a moderate oven for about half an hour.

OR, A WAR OF THE HOUSEHOLD.

By the Author of "The Double Life," "The I.lo.iliff's Scheme," &c., &e.

CHAPTER XI. THE SERPENT AMONG THE FLOWERS.

The private a.partments of the Lady Barbara comprised a suite r.,E three rooms on the eastern front of the mansion, and overiooixing the sea. They were on the second floor, directly over the drawing-room, and opened oil a wide corridor which ran parallel to the main hall, and which was connected with the latter by a narrow passage. Lord Champney hurried along this passage, and made his way to the front room of the suite ; the room which he well remembered as having been in former years the sitting-room or boudoir of his wife.

_ The door was closed. He knocked loudly. No one answered his summons. Then he turned the knob impatiently, pushed open the door, and strode into the room. It was a wide, high chamber, with a great bay- window to the north—a continuation of the draw- ing-room bay—with four long windows, one of them a quaint oriel, looking to the sea. The room was known in the house as the "oriel parlour," but it being the private room of the baroness, no one, not even her niece, dared to visit it without an express invitation from its mistress. At the moment of Lord Chatnpney's unceremoni- ous entrance the room was unoccupied. His lord- ship, therefore, halted near the threshold and looked around him with singular curiosity. The furniture had all been changed, and the very character of the room altered, since he had last visited it.

The floor was covered with a velvet carpet, which resembled a bed of cool, thick, green mosses, upon whose surface had been carelessly flung great white pond-lilies. The walls were hung with pictures. An open piano stood in a farther corner; portfolios of engravings abounded; a carved book- case was filled with choice volumes, and soft lounges and tempting easy-chairs filled the various nooks and niches.

Sliding doors connected this apartment with the dressing-room adjoining. These doors were at this moment slightly ajar, and Lord Champney's wandering glances beheld through the aperture the Lady Barbara, seated by a window, the blinds of which were drawn, and apparently absorbed in the contemplation of some small object which she held in her hand. _ _

Involuntarily he moved toward the connecting doors, but as abruptly halted, almost at its thres- hold. •

For the Lady Barbara's tears were falling on the object now, which Lord Champney recognised as a small, incased photograph. Unconscious of the fiercely blazing eyes fixed upon her, the lady pressed the picture to her lips with a passionate tenderness that went to her hus- band's heart, and then thrust it in her bosom, as if that were its habitual resting-place. Lord Champney gave utterance to a sharp, quick hiss, that rustled through the room like the warn- ing of a startled and infuriated serpent. The Lady Barbara sprang up in affright. At the same moment her eyes met the burning gaze of her husband.

In an instant she had grown deadly pale. " You here? "she cried, in a haughty amazement, putting her hand to her Ifeart. "It seems so," sneered her husband. - The Lady Barbara drew herself up proudly. "By what right do you intrude into my private rooms, Lord Champney ? " she demanded. "By the right of a husband, of course," returned the baron, with a sardonic smile more terrible than louder expressions of anger. "I believe lam your husband still, madam, in the eyes of the law, even if you have lost sight of the fact." The Lady Barbara made no answer. Lord Champney pushed open wide one of the sliding doors, excatiining : • "Come out here. I have something to say to you." The Lady Barbara obeyed, coming out into the oriel parlour. "Now let us understand each other, madam," said her husband, waving the bouquet he held after an imperious fashion. "1 have not come here to see you out of any impulse of affection. That, thank the fates, is all dead between us." "Dead !

"Yes. Did you think my love had survived all these years ?" mied Lord Champney, with a sneer. "it is true I did romance a little the day I came here, but you interpreted my words at the time at their true value. So far from loving you, Lady Champney—so far from being the inconsolable victim of your cruelty-1 should reject any over- tures from you, should you choose to humble yourself to me. Ido not desire even a friendship with you. I care nothirg whatever for you. But, undeistand me, 1 do care for my honour, and I will defend it against all the world.' "A very laudable resolve," said the Lady Bar- bara, coldly. "Did you intrude here merely to say that?" The baron's cheeks flushed with a sullen red. "That and more," he said, darkly._

"Au," said the baroness, coolly. Your manner grows theatrical. Its a pity you were born rich, my lord. The stage would have had a bright orna- ment if you had gone upon it! But, if I may be allowed to ask, why waste this scene upon me? What have I to do with your resolves ?" " Your affectation of ignorance is hardly natural 1" ejaculated Lord Champoey. "I am your guaraian, madam, and I intend to see that you do not dis- grace me." "Disgrace you ?" cried the Lady Barbara, with the air of an insulted queen. "Those were the words I used, madam. I came in here to see you concerning another matter, and arrived just in time to witness the highly edifying spectacle you presented while weeping over and kissing the likeness of some villain—the very like- ness that lies next your heart at this moment, madam. As your husband and guardian, I demand to see that picture." The bright scarlet rushed in quick waves into the Lady Barbara's cheeks, like waves of blood upon snow. she put her hands against her breast, as if to defend herself against assault. "You demand an impossibility," she said, huskily. "you refuse to show me that picture, then? You defy me?' " Yes, ' said the Lady Barbara, in a low, broken voice. "Do you know what this refusal proclaims you? Do you understand what it makes you hencefor- ward in my•sight ?" The wife was silent. "I can forgive you for hating me," continued his lordship, his eyes glowing like beacon fires, "but I cannot forgive this falseness, this treachery. Show me that picture, or from this moment I shall regard you, in my own mind, as a lost and aban- doned woman 1" _ _

The Lady Barbara had seemed to hesitate, as if she were tempted to yield to his demand. But now her outraged pride rose like a bulwark between her and her husband, and she rejoined, coldly: "You have no right to insist upon the surren- der to you of a secret upon whose existence you stumbled in so treacherous a manner! Cease your insults, Lord Champney. lam unfortunate in be- ing bound to you in marriage, but I assure you I am no happier than yourself, and that I would give all I have to be free ! "

The baron's face suddenly whitened. "You'll have to wait for freedom till I am dead !" he sneered. "Then you can marry whom you will. But while I live, you shall not disgrace outwardly either yourself or me I" The Lady Barbara sat down by a window, and drew up the lower blind as if for air. "You utterly decline to show me the picture ? '' demanded Champney, resting one arm and the bouquet upon the end of the piano. " Utterly !" .. ______ _

"This secret, then, is another gulf between us ! " be said, sternly. "1 have nothing more to say on the subject. But a reconciliation between us is impossible until you come to me in a wifely humility and place that picture in my hands I " The Lady Barbara laughed scornfully. "Then we shall never be reconciled " she said. "So be it. And now to come to the object of my visit. lam the bearer of a bouquet—" His wife turned her head slowly. "You meant it for me 1" she asked

In spite of her anger and pride, a sudden light and warmth glowed in her rare violet eyes. She fancied that her husband had intended the flowers for a peace-offering. "They were for you," replied the baron. "Then give them to me Sidney," said the Lady Barbara, more kindly. "How lovely they are ! You remembered how fond I am of flowers ?"

Without a word, Lord Champney, crossed the floor and laid the bouquet in her lap. Then he stood near her, leaning against the mantel-piece,

and watching her with a curl us smile. The lady toyed with the lovely blossoms, which had begun already to droop under the rough treat- ment they had received at the hands of the baron. And, after a little, she perceived, as her husband had done, the tiny note hidden, like a serpent, among the flowers. She started, and looked up in wonder and a sud- den apprehension. "Take it out !" said Champney, sternly She obeyed. "Read it I" commanded his lordship. _

The Lady Barbara turned the little note over, a shy smile gathering about her mouth. She thought in her heart that the baron had written her this note in a remorseful tenderness.

"How differently the interview might have ended if I had not been looking at that picture," she thought, sorrowfully. "He meant, perhaps, to be reconciled. Well, that is all past now. He no longer loves me." She sighed, and the faint smile faded from her face. There was no address on the note. It was sealed with white wax, and was, in its outward looks, as dainty as the receptacle in which her ladyship had found it.

With trembling fingers, the Lady Barbara tore open the missive. It contained a closely written page. The lady started back, recoiling as if the letter contained a serpent. "Well ?" said his lordship, coolly. " I—l recognise the handwriting," murmured the wife, faintly. "Of course you do," sneered the baron. "I supposed you would. But go on. Read the letter."

"I cannot I I will not !" cried the Lady Barbara, crumpling the missive between her fingers. "I insist upon it. If you refuse, you will only more fully confirm my convictions. You know the handwriting, and it would appear that you know the contents of the letter before having read it. That argues a state of rapport between you and the writer. Are you afraid that I shall want to read the letter? If you do not read it, I will."

Terrified by this threat, the Lady Barbara un- folded the letter and read it, the words dancing strangely on the tinted page, as if they had been so many mocking, live things. When she had finished its perusal, her cheeks whitening strangely, Lord Champney said: "You have read it, then? Shall I read it also ?" The wife shcok her head.

" Barbara," said his lordship, bending nearer to her, in a sorrowful sternness, " if you are innocent of downright falsity and wrong-doing, you will place that letter in my hands. If any one has in- sulted you in that letter, I will avenge your wrongs. If any one has written to you in pursu- ance of what is called a 'flirtation,' having pre- sumed upon your recent unprotected condition, I shall pardon you and punish 'dm. Barbara," and his voice rose passionately, "if you are innocent, show me that letter." "I cannot." A groan burst from the baron's lips. His wife could have echoed it with a greater despair. She had been forced by her enemies into a cruel situation—the most cruel conceivable.

The !atter was from the Colonel Effingham to whom she had alluded in her conversation with Wil:ard Ames.

The dastardly writer—as if with a view, one might imagine, to this very scene--had begun by addressing her in the loudest words of love, and had thankel her for the encouragement she had given him ; expressed his gratitude at having learaed that she returned his ardent affection, t-nd had begged her to meet him at a certain spot near the sea, on her own grounds, " where we met the last time ;" and concluded by assuring her that he had heard of her husband's return, but that the writer wouki continue to elude or quiet any sus- picions the baron might entertain of the actual state of affars. To this precious document was appended the name of Albert Effingharri

A more diabolical lei-ter had never been con- ceived, nor one more likely to utterly madden Lord Champney. The Lady Barbara, familiar as she had been with many of the dissolute colonel's proceedings, was utterly amazed at the unparalloled audacity and falsity of this missive. But all her anger, annoyance and righteous in- dignation were swallowed up in her great fear lest, after all, it should fall into her husband's hands.

This Colonel Effingham was an egregious cox- comb, vain, heartless and unprincipled. He had been formerly a devoted admirer of the Lady Barbara, but her marriage, and his own, had come between them for many years. His wife's death, and the Lady Barbara's loneliness and un- protected condition, had at last turned his thoughts back to her, and, as she had said, he had annoyed her greatly by sending her flowers and letters, and in haunting at times her grounds, so that she could not go out for a stroll without meet- ing him. And fin.lly he had added to his persecutions this crowning infamy.

To exhibit the letter to the baron would be but to pre, ipitate some terrible calamity, to arouse a scandal, and to nause all England to ling with her husband's name and her own. For the Lady Bar- bara knew that these false words would be to her husband's fiery spirit like a fining brand thrown into the midst of gunpowder. In the face of that letter, she knew that any pro- testations of her innocence would be laughed to scorn.

To refuse to show the lying document would be scarcely better. What should she do?

A tumult raged in her soul. Her brain seemed on fire. Her heart beat thickly, with muffled pul- sations.

"Well ?" said Lord Champney, grimly. "Will you give me the letter ?" " 6idney," his wife whispered, clutching the let- ter closely, a strange dizziness seizing her, "sup- pose it was a love-letter. Suppose it alluded to former letters, and to—to meetings. Suppose it made me seem false to you, aid yet I should declare the letter a base lie, would you believe me V'

" No !" thundered the husband, beside himself with passion. "No man would wtite such a letter to a pure and modest woman. No man would make such assertions in a letter to a woman unless they were true. Are there auch statements in that letter r

The Lady Barbara forced a quivering smile. " That question to me ?" she asked, trying to speak haughtily. "I was puttiog F 1 Case to you. Have I fallen so low in your estimation that you could suspect me of receiving such letters as the one I described ?"

"No; you have not yet." "Then I Dever shall !" cried the bAroness, arous- ing herself, and looking up at him strangely. Before he could speak or hinder her, she delib- erately tore the letter into a hundred fragments and tossed them out of the Window.

The vagrant breeze caught them up, whirled them into an outgoing current of air, and they m ere carried out seaward.

The Lady Barbara tossed the bouquet after them, and then sank back into her seat. " What am I to understand from that strange movement ?" demanded Lord Clp,mpney. "Simply that I decline to make a cui.tidaut of you, my lord !" "I am to think the worst, then ?" " You may think what you please !" "At least, tell me who wrote that letter ?" " it Never. The secret is mine, and shall keep ."

The baron smothered an imprecation. His eyes blazed luridly. "It is all over between us!" he exclaimed. "I dislike you as much as I once loved you. Love and contempt cannot dwell together. I shall never make any further advan..:es to you, madam. I regret that you are my wife, but I have no intention of committing suicide that you may be free. Neither will I sue for a divorce, or go back to Germany. In short, Barbara, I shall stay at Salton-, as your guardian and protector, and make it my care to preserve in honour your good name, since you have proved yourself incompetent to the task. Thele- fore be cautious. My eye will beewr on you. I intend, first of all, to discover the identity of this secret lover of yours. If it's Ellhounn," and his lordship's face grew yet darker, •• he shall p..y for his insolence in blood ! And now, madam," he concluded, hiding his awful grief under a cover of mockery, " permit me to wish you good-niornitig He bowed deeply, and went out. The Lady Pmrbro.ra tuttFu•ed to the doer, lucked it, and fell fainting on the floor.

NEW LIFE FOR A LADY.

HOW A MANCHESTER LADY LIVES.

A pair of pleasant brown eye,, loo,king out of an ex- pressive face, writes a Stockport Advertiser reporter, were the most prominent feature-. of the lady with whore- I chatted the other day at No. 51, Rusholme-road, Ardwick, Manchester. Mrs. Woodruff—such was her name—l found had recently come out of a long stretch of ill-health and misery into a new life, bright with promise, and already full of renewed strength. As her two bonny fair•haired children played about her knee, she told me the story of her fifteen years' sickness. "I am told that you are suffering from a wasting die' ease, Mrs. Woodruff."

"Yes, I have. I got so low that it was too much trouble to do anything. The weakness became so great that I was unable to look after my family or myself, and the house was full of misery." "How long did you suffer ?" " About fiftcen years. I tried private doctors, hospitals, and patent medicines, one after another, but neither seemed to do me any real good. I attended St. Mary's Hospital, Manchester, as an out-patient and was an in-patient in the Clifford-street Hospital here. But the wasting continued to undermine my strength, and I was at a loss what to do. I kept on' getting worse, until, about three months age, I was prs. vailed upon to try Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale' People. Before I had finished the first box, I felt a great improvement, and now I am quite well again, my strength has come back, and I feel ready to go any' where and do anything. Dr. Williams' Pills, and they alone, cured me."

"And after three -months you still feel well P" "Yes, quite well and strong. Before I took the rills. everything I had to do seemed a trouble: I was very languid, and life was a burden. Often I could not go • out because of my extreme weakness."

Mrs. Woodruff is now in splendid health, thanks to the tonic virtue of Dr. Williams' Pink Pills, which cure not only cases like the above, but those of low vitality, consumption, locomotor ataxy, sciatica, palpitations, and the disorders which arise from impoverished bloodr such as anemia, pale and sallow complexion, weakness,- Jowl of appetite, shortness of breath, pains in the back, nervous headache, early decay, all forms of female weakness, and wasting strength from any cause. STUFFY CHURCHES.

Most persons who attend church with some regular. ity, especially crowded ones, have often experienced a feeling of drowsiness during the service, which coeld not have been caused by the dulness of the sermon. Experi- ence has says 'Science Siftings," shown that it is soon dissipated on going into the fresh air. The cause of this drowsiness has generally been attributed to a lack of oratory on the part of the minister, or to his dulness,hr prosy ways of thinking. A hygienist will, in most eases, track its causP to the impurity of the air of the church. In too many cases those who build sacred edifices have no conception of the amount of fresh air required to keep the atmosphere of a room sweet and clean. It cap, however. be easily calculated. There should be not lag than 3,000 cubic tePt of outside air provided hourly for each person while in the church. If much less is sup plied the cartoonic acid and other toxic matters given oft by respiration increase so fast that in a crowded roora the air becomes too foul for suppoiting life healtlafullV in a very few minutes. We cannot expect to keep the air in our rooms as pure as out-door air, but there is a standard of purity which we should try to maintain. Out-door air contains frohl three to four parts of carbonic acid in every 10,000 parta of air. This we may breathe with iniipunity. hut in- door air contains more, and much more, even 10 parts, and in a crowded placP.as in some school-rooms,theatree, arid churches, 15 or 20 parts. If, however, there are more than 6 parts in 10,000 of air it has a deptessin.g effect on the neryious system and produces drowsi- ness.

Such an air, if heated, as in cold weather, affects akin the preacher. Clergyman's sore throat and hearsenesi are induced by the constant effort to speak well in a heated and relaxing atmosphere. Church windows, eV cent in rural districts, are not made to open ; and, ever if they were, unless the entering air is directed upWnrug to a considerable height, it falls upon the heads of the congregation, and complaints of draughts are made, which promptly sezure their closing. Most churches art: heated by hot air furnaces, and this brings in much fres air if property arranged, but never enough. Exhaust ventilators in the rocf are PractinilY unknown in churches; conEequently the foul and heated air never escapes, and alter service, as the heated air cools,it. descends,and a fresh congregation rebreathes the used air of its predecessors. Our churches are intended i as places n which to keep alive the moral sentiments and to increase our knowledge as to how our conduct should be regulated by principles of r;ght and duty, and we are beginning to find out that morals may be related tc our physical conduct, and that it is a sin against thS laws of our being to live and breathe a polluted atmoS• phere even in a house of worship. The clergy, who are' as deeply interested as any in this subject may most properly act as leaders in reforming our church air to a standard which hygiene demands.

THE BANKRUPTCY OF THE

WARRINGTON SWINDLER.

PRISONER AT WARRINGTON.

At the Bankruptcy Court, Bank-street, Warrington, on Friday John Garlick alias John Grant, was brought up under an order made by his Honour Judge Wynne Ffourees from gaol to undergo his public examination in bankruptcy. He appeared before Mr. Registrar Ridgway, and was questioned by Mr. Potter (resistant official receiver). Creditors of the debtor were repre- sented by Mr. Longland, Mr. Woods, Mr. Browne and Mr. Longton. In answer to questions by the Assistant Official Receiver, witness said the answers he had made to the printed questions were correct to the best of his belief. With regard to the £6O lent by Stockton the prisoner said that Stockton was partly paid, and if he didn't get full value it was his own fault and not the fault of witness. This answer caused some mirth in the court, and the prisoner addressing the Registrar said he was there to answer any fair question but he was not there to be laughed at. Proceeding to answer the questions put to him, he said it was not cor- rect that he drew £1 a week from the business. He did not draw that amount but only £5 or £6 altogether. How did you live P—My wife keret the house on.— What is your full name ? John Garlick.—You have traded in Warington in the name of John Grant. Will you explain how that came about?— Reasons connected with the police.—What reasons ? He did not think he was justified in explaining it pub- licly. He would do so privately.—The Registrar having said that the prisoner must answer the questions put to him, he said he was a ticket-of-leave man in the name of John Garlick and therefore to avoid being known, he changed it to John Grant. That was the reason.—Have you traded in any other name ?—Yes, as John Johnson and John Henry.—When were you known as John Johnson P—About eleven or twelve years ago at Blackburn.—What was your reason for it ?—To pre- vent being known by the nolice.—When were you known as John Henry P—ln 1885.—Where ?—Stoke-on-Trent. —Why ?—For the same reason as the others.—Did you trade in the name of John Johnson at Stoke ?—No, in the name of John Henry.—l understand you are under- going twelve months imprisonment at Walton for obtain- ing money by false pretences from Mrs. Ashmore ?—I was convicted for obtaining money by false pretences from William Stockton. The case brought against me by Mrs. Ashmore was quashed.—You were convicted on July 14th ?—Yes.--Where there any other charges brought against you besides that by Stockton ? —Yes, Mrs. Ashmore brought a charge but the counts of the inducement was not supported by the evidence. Were there any other charges ?—None as I am aware of. —You have been in prison before I think ?—Yes.— Where ?—Prisoner (to the Registrar) : Am Ito answer that question ?—The Registrar : Yes.—Prisoner : Preston, Stafford, Liverpool, and Minehester.—Will you tell me what your business career has been. When did you start P—Twenty years ago.—That would be in 1876? —About that time.—Where did you start ?—At Bolton. —What as P—Timber merchant, joiner, etc.—How long did that last ?—Perhaps twelve months.—Did you pay your creditors in full then ?—No, not quite, but they were pretty nearly all paid. There are perhaps £7O or £BO owing besides a sum of money I have had from my mother.—What was the end of it P—An execution wig served.—W hat became of you ?—I cleared out.—l sup- pose you mean absconded ?—Yes. I suppose se.—Where did you go to P—l went to work as a journeyman for Mesesre. Neill and Son, of Manchester.—That brings usl to 1879. What did you do next P—l went to Blackburn. Did you trade there ?—Yes, as a broker and furniture dealer P—Did you ply your creditors in full ?-leFewith the exception of £7 or £3.—Why didn't you pay P—Be-, cause I hadn't the means.—How did it come to an end ? —lt didn't pay and I was short of capital.—What did you do next ?—I went to Bolton again.—What business did you engage in?—That of a general broker.—Did you trade in your own name ?—Yes.—How long were you a broker there P—About two years.—Did you pay your debts ?—I left owing about £3O or £4O —Why did you leave P—For family reasens.—Where did you go to ? I believe I went to Stoke-on-Trent.--That would be in 1894. Were you not a prisoner in 1892 ?—I served six months in Manchester.—What for P—For obtaining 3s. 71. worth of firewood by false pretences.—Were you convicted in the name of Grant ?—No, Garlick.—When did yob go to Stoke P—ln 1884.—Did you trade under an assumed name there ?—Yes.—Why did you do so ? I can hardly tell you. I think it was because I was afraid of them following me. I had been oonvicted in the name of Garlick and I did not want anyone to know I had been convicted.—What were you engaged in there ? —As metal refiner and artificial stone maker.—How long did you stop?—Three or four months,—Whydid you stop P—l was stopped by the police for obtaining goods by false pretences. Were you convicted ?—Yes.—What was the sentence P—Twenty months hard labour.— Where at P—Stafford Assizes.—ln what name P—John ,IHenry.—Was your true name then disclosed ?—Yes.— When you came out of prison what did you do ?—I was re-arrested.—What for P—Obtaining £3 at Bolton by false pretences.—When did you obtain that amount ? About the year 1883 —Were you convicted ? Yes, and was sentenced to five years penal servitude.— Where ? At Bolton sessions.—When did you come out P—ln 1889. —What did you do then P—l commenced business at Preston in the name of John Garlick.—What was the nature of the business P—Manufacturing shop-keepers goods.—Where at P—Marsh-lane. Preston.—For how long P—About two months.—Why did you stop ?—I was stopped by the police on a charge of obtaining goods by false pretences, and arcpritted.—Where did you go to next ?—Blackburn.—Hew long did you remain there?— About 2-.1- years.—What did you eugage in ?—I com- menced manufacturing furniture.—At what place ? Litherton Alley.—Where all your liabilities at Preston paid ?—I don't know. It was handed over to the credi- tors and they did what they liked.—Well with regard to the furnishing business ?—I had only just commenced it.—What happened ?—I was stopped by the police.— Was a charge preferrede against you ?—Yes, for obtain- ing money by false pretences.—Were you convicted ? Yea, I received a sentence of twelve months imprison- ment.—That brings you down to 1892. What did you do before you entered Wigan in 1895.-4 was re-arrested on finishing the term of imprisonment, and sent back to finish the balance of the five years sentence.—When did you come out P—Ore May 8, 1895.—Y0u then went to Wigan. What did you de there ?—I commenced step- ladder making at Acton's Walk.—How long were you there P—Two or three months.—What was the end ?—I left the town because it was an open secret tbat I was a ticket-of-leave man. I could not for shame to stop.— Did you pay all your debts there ?—No.—Have you scheduled the debts now ?—Yes.—When did you leave Wigan P—July 20, 1895.—What did you do after that ? —I took a contract from the Wigan and Neaten Colliery Company to repair wagons.—Did you eomplete the con- tract P—No sir.—Where did you go to next P—Warring- ton.—No liabilities arose out of the contract P—No.— When did you come te Warrington?—ln November or December of 1895.—Y0u state you commenced business in February, 1898.—That is correct.—What did yen do in the months before that ?—I worked as a journeyman joiner for Mr. Collins and Mr. Williams.—Have you given us a full, true, and complete account of your trading career?— I think so.—Were you never at Leicester ?—Yes, sir.— Were you convicted there ?—Yes.—What for P—For a fraudulent bailee.—When was that P—ln 1886.—What were the circumstances of the • case ?—I hired a market cart and sold it.—You hadn't been trading P—Well I had been hawking.— Did you contract any debts at Leices- ter P—lt was not in Lsicester but in Begworth, a small place in Leicestershire.—Were there any liabilities °wine? —About £8 or £lo.—Were they discharged ?—No, but I left them everything I had ?—What was the sentence there 2—Six months imprisonment.and I was re-arrested at the prison gates.—You have stated that you com- menced business at Warriugton with £7O or £BO. Where did you get it from ?—lt was profits made out of the contract, money saved in Warrington and the rest bor- rowed money.—You commenced as a furniture manufac- ture and a timber merchant. Had you any previous experience as a furniture maker ?—Yes, as a journeymaa.—Had you any previous experience in the timber trade ?—Yes, sir,it was part of my trade. —To whom did you intend to,eiell timber ?—To anyone I could.—Can you produce any evidence to show that this business was a bona-fide business ?--I mild produce evidence to so sr that I could have had more credit if I had needed it.—What part did you take in it P—l first worked as a joiner, and when the business increased I changed my clothes and superintended.—Your liabili- ties are stated at £941 14s. 6d. and the assets estimated to realise £231 135.7 d. Do you think you havescheduled all your liabilities ?—I think so.—l am informed that they are likely to amount to over £l,lOO. Does that sur- prise you ?—Yes, it does rather surprise me.—You have scheduled over 100 creditors ; can you explain how it is that you have such a large number P—Simply because I wanted the goods for the purpose of carrying on the business.—What were the causes of your insol- vency ? In the first instance through the men not getting on 'with their work quick enough, and in the second place because I could not get sufficient men to work the timber up, and also irasuffi !lent capital. —Tbe books don't show the business transactions in the form of a balance sheet. Do you suggest that your books show any statement of assets arid liabilities ? No, sir.—Why didn't you keep your books properly ? Lapse of memory and insufficient time to attend to them.—Did you ever take stock ?—Yes, roughly.— Where are the stock sheets ?--They were in the hands of

The prisoner was cross-examined at consider- able length with reference to his absconding from the town. He stated that when he was arrested at Pember- ton he had not made up his mind to abscond. He ad- mitted that he packed up the greater part of his goods and sent them to the station in the name of Seeker and consigned them to Wapping. He admitted that he wrote two letters on May 12th, one to Mr. Roberts, and one to Mrs. Ashmore, whom he called his wife, and enclosing the key of his premises. The prisoner was also subjected to a searching examination by Mr. Longland, Mr. Woods, Mr. Longton and Mr. Browne on behalf of the creditors. At the con- clusion, the Assistant Official Receiver applied for the examination to be adjourned sine die. This was agreed to by the Registrar. The prisoner then said he had an application to make with reference to the goods which were sent away from the house in F lrshaw-st. He understood they had been sold by Mr. Worthington and realised £l4. He applied for that money to be handed over to Mrs. Ashmore.— The Assistant Official Receiver said he thought that claim would be resisted by the trustees.—The proceed- ings then terminated.

WARRINGTON TEC lINICA.L INSTITUTE.

THE CONTINUATION SCHOOL-DISTRIBUTION OF

PRIZES.

The prizes and certificates gained in the last session by the students of the Continuation School were distributed by Councillor Henry Roberts, J.P. (deputy-chairman of the Technical Instruction Committee), at the People's College on Monday evening. Amongst tho:e present were Mr. Twiss (headmaster of People's College), Mr. Hall (headmaster of the Continuation School), Major McGarty, Mr. Corradine and Mr. Charles Madeley (secre- tary of the Technical Institute). At the outset the report of the Continuation School ' for the previous session was read by the Secretary. It ran as follows :—" We are pleased to be able to report that during the last session the continuation school I again made an advance upon its position in the previous I year. But the increase this time is only small. The 'number of pupils who entered last autumn was 399, be- ing 31 more than those who joined in 1894. It may be worth while to give the number who joined for each I separate subject :—Arithmetic 359, writing and compo- sition 371, shorthand 152, drawing 229, elements of science 56. History and mensuration were also offered, but the numbers entering them were so:small that the subjects were dropped. These classes were of course open to both sexes. Of the 96 girls who joined,64 took needle work and 45 cookery. The attendance during the session was better on the whole proportionate to the number of entries, and altogether we may say that the pupils as a rule joined earlier in the session, and came to the school oftener than they had done in the year before. This, the committee feels, is an evidence that the value of the school, and the importance of joining it,are begin- ning to be more deeply felt amongst these for whom it is intended. The prizes for attendance, which were of- fered by the committee, were gained by 99 pupils, 89 of whom qualified for the first class prize by making 100 attendances on 50 days. As in previous years, the winner in each case had the privilege of selecting his own prize, and in some cases, took the opportunity of obtain- ing a book or a box of instruments of higher value, by contributing to the increased cost. The number of can- didates who sat at the examinations of the Union of Lancashire and Cheshire Institute in the spring, was practically the same as last year, with a slight increase, correaponding to the increase in the school generally. For the pupils who did best in these examinations the committee has awarded the following scholarships of the value of 10s. each, Mary Ward, David B. Clarke ; seven of the value of 7s. 61. each, J. J. Baskerville, Percy B. Gandy,Ernest Kendrick,Mary A. Lea,George A. Williams Georgina Barlow, Florence McGarty ; eight of the value of Sc. each, John L. Hawthorn, Bertha London, James MeWhirter, John Charles Mason, Edith Turner, Emily Houghton, Basil Baker, Frank IBullen and Esther McGarty. :The holders of these seholarships will be required to take such classes in the continuation school or the higher departments as may be approved and to pay the usual class fees, the amount of the scholarship being paid them on compli- ance with the usual rules as to attendance and examina- tion. The school was examined in drawing by the Science and Art Department, and was awarded the highest grade "Excellent." In addition, 19 scholars ob- tained cards of merit in the drawing examination, and the committee had decided that each of these, under certain conditions, may attend the School of Art fer this session as a free student. Fivelantern lectures were de- livered by Mr. Hall during the session onthe following sub- jects:—"The British Empire." Across the United States," "A peep into nature through the microscope," "Under- ground Life," "Popular Authors." In conclusion, the Committee recognise that for the efficient management of the Continuation School, the excellent instruction given and the good results that generally attend this department of the Technical Institute, it is greatly indebted to the excellent work done by Mr. Hall, and the assistant teachers."

Councillor ROMIRTS, previous to distributing the prizes, delivered a brief address in which he gave some good advice to the students. He said that the duty he had to perform was one that was extremely pleasant to him, and one that would be extremely pleasant to many of these present. Ple.was very glad to see how remark- ably successful—judging from the numbers there that night and the report which had just been read —those education classes had been. He should not be doing his duty as a member of the Techni- cal Instruction Committee if he did not publicly express their warm feeling of admiration for the way Mr. Hall and his staff had conducted those classes during the last two or three sessions. (Applause.) He would have been glad if more of the parents had been present because the committee wanted to bring home to the fathers and mothers and the people of Warrington the responsibility which they had put upon them in re- gard to the higher education of their children. He re- membered very well—and he supposed he might be privileged to give a little advice—that when he had left school some few years he realised for the first time bow much he had forgotten. Being an old People's College lad, and proud of the fact, he was very pleased indeed to have an opportunity for the first time of addressing a meeting in the People's College, and trying to say some- thing which might interest the lads there. He wished them to be determined, as far as 'hey possibly could, that they would fill a more important position than their fathers had done before them. He was sorry he could not say that which was specially applicable to the young ladies but he hoped they would draw by inference from what he should say to the boys, and apply it to them• selves as far as it was possible. The immortal bard through the medium of one of his characters had told them to "fling away ambition" en the ground that by it the :angels fell. He (Councillor Roberts) did not believe that was Shakes- peare's belief, for if it was, with all due deference to his. better knowledge and ability, he should say it was decidedly wrono. He would not give a fig for a lad who was devoid of ambition. If there was a youth before him that night who had not the slightest idea or determina- tion to be something better than his father was it was about time he had it krocked into him one way or another. The terrible amount of competition in the world in places of employment end in trades of all kinds made it absolutely necessary that whatever brains God had endowed them with, and whatever opportunities they had put before them, they should make the best possible use of those brains or they would fall behind in the race they were taking part in.He would say to every one therte,"Make up your mind that you will not let any opportunity pass of improving your education in every subject you may try to do." They never knew but that a little knowledge on a special subject might be of great service to them, and perhaps the turning point in making their career a great deal more successful than it would have been. He did not mean to say that all the time one had at his disposal should be spent in further work. They knew very well that" all work and no play makes Jack a dull boy." but they must reason these things out in a proper and fair spirit and provide a fair amouet of recreation. Still there was plenty of time in which the improvement of the mind and acquisi- tion of knowledge might be acquired. It was a very pleasant thing to be able to feel that one had got a long evening,to be spent as one liked. That might be right in its way, but don't let them think there was much advantage to be gained in sauntering about the streets and lanes, sin-iking cigarettes at two a penny—(laiighter)—or perhaps even cheaper than that. Cigarettes were now at such a low price that it seemed the duty of butchers' boys and others to smoke when they were doing their errands. He did not think if any lad knew his employer's eye was upon him when he was doing so that he would feel it was very probable that his promotion would take place at an early date. He mentioned that as showing how a great deal of money and time was spent which might be better employed. He did not come there to lecture them but to encourage them in what it seemed they were de- termined to go en with. Some might say that others had better opportunities. That was true, and it often happened that those who had the best opportunities did not make the best use of them. He would recommend those present to read a book, which could be obtained from the Library, written by Samuel Smiles, entitled " Self Help." They would find there numerous instanced of men who had made the r names known through- out the world in one sensie,and who had sprung from the bottom of the !ander to the very top in the various trades in which they were engaged and whose opportunities were far worse than those of any boy in that room. They had made the best use of their opportunities and the re- ts u t wa s their otw ru, uc,h prosperitytheirn eanlsdrg itehg. rrh e prosperity e object o of hnationitself hf the Continuation School was to give !hose who had left ts c he yd e ohl earlyn obtained en opp ytttuh nei typ public improvingelem eni elementary knowledgeshosi and h prepare them to join the Technical classes. a little later on,,and also for those who had a desire to pin the Tech- nical Classes tint who did not. feel quite ready for doing Rn. These would find the Continuation School useful as a stepping !tone for something better. He hoped the Continuation School would be continued for many 'Tars to come in order that its full usefulness might be brought out to the advantage of the town generally. He was a native of this good old town, and there was net such another place in the world like it to him. (Laughter.) They were all very proud of it, but they would have still greater pride in it if it was better than it was. One way to make it so was for the generation now coming for- ward to take the opportunities that presented them- selves, and make the most of them so as to put their present town a long way in the shade in every possible respect. (Applause.)

The Prizee were then distributed, and at the close, Major McGarty proposed a vote of thanks to Mr. Roberts for his services.—Mr. Corradine in secondine, said he envied every student present for the opportunities which they now possessed for obtaining knowledge. PRESENTATION TO A WORK'S MANAGER AT WARRINGTON.

There was an interesting event in one of the work- rooms of Messrs. Richmond and Company, Limited, en Friday night,when the esteemed manager of the works— Mr. H. C. Ransome—was presented with some tokens of the respect in which he is held by all connected with the firm on the occasion of his recent marriage. Light refreshments were served in what is known as the "tinning "shop, at which the workmen,fresh from their employment, were present. Amongst those present on the platferm were Mr. and Mrs. Richmond, Mr. and Mrs. Ransome, Miss Ransome, Mr. L. A. Voisey, Mr. W. S. Gilbert (London), Mr. J. W. Slack, Mr. S. A. Scrive- ner Mr. C. R. Adamson, Mr. E. W. Allen, Mrs. H. M. Ashton, Mrs. T. Poole, Mr. Hurst, and Mr. Smith. After a violin and pianoforte duet by Mr. R. [l. Pear- son and Mr. W. H. Payton _ _ . .

Mr.RICHMOND said It was with very great pleasure that once again he appeared before them on an occasion ot that kind. They did not all get married, and those who did generally found that once was sufficient to last them for the rest of their life. (Laughter.) He was sure they were all aware that Mr. and Mrs. Ransome had taken that step, and had taken it with the heartiest good wishes of all present. Those connected with the firm includine the directors, workmen, and staff generally had subqcribed together to celebrate that auspicious occasion. He (Mr. Richmond) thought it must be gratifying to Mr. Ran- some to feel that in the course of a little over five years he had risen as very few men were able to rise. He had earned for himself a position which all of them knew he deserved, and a position in which they were able to respect and esteem him. (Applause.) All of them w mld not remember the early days when Mr. Ransomer joined the company. He (Mr. Richmond) remembered him coming to them quite a young man. He was then very energetic and determined, and soon earned for himself a reputation as a traveller. More assistance was required at the works.and he was then put into the works, and so successful was that experiment that the board of directors hb.d entrusted him with the general management of the concern, and they would be inter- ested to know that in another year he would be invited to join the board of directors. (Applause.) Having so far earned and met the success he deserved, it was their earnest wish that he might have health in which to enjoy the success he had merited. They also hoped that with wealth and health combined they would bring happiness. They wished both Mr. and Mrs. Ransome the greatest happiness that this world could give. It was with no small feeling ef pride that he saw so many employes before him, and his mind went back to the time when they were a small number indeed. They had struggled and striven against great difficulties and almost overwhelming odds, and when their future was not as bright as it was that day. It was a great satisfaction to him to feel that during the time he had been lain aside with illness, orders kad come in great volumes and their works— although enlarged—had not been able to cope with the deliveries. It had been found necessary to drauble the size of the works and he hoped shortly they would be able to show their friends not only model ranges but model works. (Applause.) In conclusion he referred in eulo- gistic terms to the competent staff, and then asked Mr. Ransorne's acceptance of a fine Bechstein piano, a cabinet, and an over-mantel, which he stated were em- blems of the respect and esteem and good wishes they had towards both Mr. and Mrs. Ransome. (Applause.) Mr. W. S. Gilbert, Mr. Hughes, and Mr. Smith, as representing different departments of the firm, also spoke. In responding Mr. RANSOM: said he found a diffieulty in finding words to express the gratitude of his wife and himself for the generous way they had acknowledged their wedding. It was really more than they expected, and he thanked everyone most heartily for those hand- some presents. As a married man he had found out how expensive housekeeping was. (Laughter.) But he.felt he had taken a step higher. (Applause.) He thanked Mr. Richmond personally for his kind remarks, which were exceedingly gratifying to him. Mrs. Raicsoarg also briefly thanked the subscribers for the present which had been made to her husband and herself. The proceeclinga terminated with some instrumental selections by Mr. R. H. Pearson and Mr. W. El. Payton

The journalist tour in South Africa is now inevit- able, because there is so much to be made out of special knowledge,and there are so many capitalists wanting to run papers of their own. It may be hazardous to mention names, but it is said that competition in a weekly direction would not have arisen here but for a certain journey to Africa and its consequences, legal and otherwise. Now one hears that another journalist of note, lately editing an evening paper, is out at the cape, cramming his notebook with facts and impressions, possibly with a view to some new enterprise. When anyone can write a full account of South African gold, in its effects upon London newspaper enterprise and Lon- don opinion, he will have some strange stories to tell.

WARRINGTON CRICKET CLUB.

BATTING AVERAGES AND BOWLING ANALYSES. The season just concluded by the above club shows that of the 21 matches played ten have been won, six lost and five drawn. Victories have been gained over Booklands (2), Sefton (2). Preston, Bootle, Llandudno, Cheadle Hulme, Northern (Liverpool), and Captain Plomer's team, whilst defeats have been sustained at the hands of Manchester, Liverpool, Preston, Bolton, Bootle, and Cheetham Hill. Rain interfered with both matches with Little Lever, whilst the return games with Cheet- ham Hill and Cheadle Huline were spoiled through a like cause. THE REV. REV. JOHN McNEILL.

In view of the forthcoming visit of this well-known Evangelist to Warrington for the purpose of holding a united mission in the Parr Hall, from October 4th to 16th, it will, says a correspondent, be interesting to eur readers to know something of the antecedents of one who, in a remarkable way, has proved himself capable of reaching the pe)ple by his evangelistic zeal and fer- vour. The Rev. John McNeill is no sensational preacher, neither does he use extravagant methods in his missions. He is a firm believer in, and preacher of the "Old, Old Stery."which,as his recent missions in Scotland,lreland, England, America, Australis and India, abundantly testify, is still a great attraction, especially when pre- sented with that tremendous earnestness so characteris- tic of the evangelist whom we now expect in our midst. _

A few particulars concerning Mr. McNeill's early life and work are appended. "I began life in the railway service," be says, "and it taught me all the best lessons of my life." He began his work days as gate boy at Inverkip Station, Renfrewshire,when he was 13 years of age. Later we find him porter at the same station, and then booking clerk, afterwards as booking clerk at Greenock and then at Edinburgh, until he retired from the railway service to go to college to study for the ministry. It was while he was engaged as an active membcr of the Edinburgh Y.M.C.A. that his zeal for miision work, his love of open air preaching, and his remarkable gifts as a preacher convinced all who knew him that he was destined for theministry. His struggle through college was a stiff battle, but he stuck to his studies with courage and determination and cleared his way as he pushed on through those eight long years by doing the work of a city missionary. As soon as his studies were finished he was offered the pastorate of the Barony Free Church, Glasgow, a fine church with one thousand members. He was also invited to Edinburgh. Re elected to begin his ministry as pastor of the McCrie Roxburgh Free Church, Edinburgh,and was ordained in the summer of 1886.

It was soon an open secret, not alone in Edinbourgh but throughout Scotland. that the new minister of McCrielßoxburgh Church was a man who would be heard —a man with a message. The quiet, half empty church was before long crowded to the doors, and the name of the young preaches on everybody's lips. Not that every- body commended him, for they did not. It was pre- dicted that the young man would run abut, he was just a comet, and would flutter down as quickly as he had blazed up. John McNeill neither ran out nor ran away; he went on preaching with increasing power, and to crowded and delighted congregations. There were sneers, jibes and ridicule from some quarters ; also charges of vulgarity,insinuations about an upstart book- kg clerk and the like. All the while the blessing of God was accompanying the burning words of the preacher. No doubt his frank, bluff home-thrusts, his merciless satire might be misunderstood or distorted by the more captious of his hearers; and possibly he did offend the superfine feelings of some. But the mists cleared ibWaV, and there stood forth a man of rare natural gifts, and with all his powers consecrated to the one high pur- pose of preaching. His work in Edinberough. his call to Regent Square Church, London, and his services there. and hie abounding labours in various parts of the world since he asked to be set free to do the work of an evange- list have fully confirmed the high promise of his early ministry. On being released from his ministerial charge at Regent Square, London, Mr. McNeill entered upon the work of the evangelist with rare earnestness and vigour. He followed me theextended series of meetings conducted by Messrs. Moody and Sankey in Scotland with many tokens of blessing, and in the principal towns the inter- est created by his addresses and sermons was quite unprecedented. Mention may be made of the meetings for business men in Glasgow. These meetings were crowded with the busy merchants and leading business men of the city, day after day, for several weeks, and that, too, though they were held at the busiest hour of the commercial man's day. The preacher's remarkable power in dealing with great gatherings of men was never more fully demonstrated than at these meetings in Glasgow. His business men's meetings in Dublin and more recently in Leeds were a repetition of those he conducted in Glasgow. The interest deepened and spread over the whole city, and immense numbers thronged to hear the Scottish evangelist. During last winter Mr. McNeill held successful mis- sions in Leeds, Sheffield, Cardiff, Scarborough, Bedford, Harrogate, &c. For the purpose of organising and carry- ing out the mission at Warrington a large special com- mittee has been formed. This committee represents the churches, chapele, missions. Sunday schools, Christian Endeavour Societies,Pleasant Sunday Afternoon Societies the Salvation Army, &c., kc. Every effort has been made to secure as full a representation as possible of the different Christian organisations in the town, and the response so far has been very encouraging. The mission is being worked from the Young Men's Christian Associa- tion, the president of which, Mr. J. Charlton Parr, J.r.. D.L., has stated his willingness to assist and support the mission. The Rector of Warrington has also written to express his sympathy with the effort. At the fist meeting of the menial committee held on Wednesday evening in the Y.M.C.A. Lecture Hall, Friars' Green, there was a large attendance. The pro- ceedings were earnest and enthusiastic. Various officers were appointed and an executive committee formed to carry out the arrangements for the mission. Mr. E. J. Guest, 11. A J.P., was elected chairman of the executive, and Mr. John Fairclough, 1.P., hon treasurer.
