NOTES AND NEWS.

One of the not least remarkable features of the Queen's reign, considering its eminently pacific charac- ter. is the enermows number of wars, "little and big," that have marked its progress. Scarcely a twelvemonth of this perhd has passed indeed without finding cu. country at war in some part of the world. The following is a list of them :—Afghan War, 1838 40 ; first China War, 1841 ; Sikh War,1845-6 ; Caffre War,1846 ; second War with China ; second Afghan War, 1849 ; second Sikh War, 1848-9; Burmese War, 1850 sacond Caffre War, 1851-2 ; second Burmese War, 1852-3 ; Crimea, 1854 ; third War with China, 1856-8 ; Indian Mutiny, 1857 ; Marini War, 1860-1 ; more wars with China, 1860 and 1862 ; second Maori War,1863-6 : Ashanti War,1864; war in Bhontan, 1864 ; Abyssinian War, 1867-8 ; war with the klazlteei, 1868 ; third Maori War, 1868-9 : war with Looshais, 1871 ; second Ashanti War,1873-4 ; third Caffre War. 1877 ; Zulu War, 1878-9 ; third Afghan War, 1878.80 ; war in B tsutoland, 1879 81 ; Transvaal War, 1879 81; Egyptian War,lBB2 ; Soudan, 1884-85-89; third Bunn ill War.lBBs-92 ; Zanzibar,lB9o ; India,lB9o; Mataleele Wars,lB94 and 1896: Chitral Campaign,lB9s; third Ashanti Campaign, 1896; second Soudan Cam- paign, 1896.

A peculiar invention purporting to have been brought forward some time since in 13.ar1in seems to have proved a reality—namely, the manufacture of horseshoes of paper, impregnated with oil or turpentine to make them waterproof, the process being described as follows : After being thus saturated, the shoe is glued together in, thin layers with a cement whieh does not become brittle when drying, being a mixture of Venetian turpentine, powdered chalk, linseed oil and lacquer ; the boles, ad- mitting the nails by which it is fastened to the hoof, are stamped through the paper when moist. The article is then subjected to very strong pressure under a hydraulic press, and when dry can be filed and planed to fit the hoof snugly.

romantic story is telil of the wife of ThormasT. Watson, the Populist candidate for the United States Tice-Presidency. It was to the efect that after one of the battles of the Civil War a richly-dressed baby was found among the dead and wounded. As no parents claimed the child,it was supposed that they bad perished in the strife, and it is not known whether they were on the Northern or the Southern side. She was adopted and cared for by a Southern soldier and his wife, until Tom Watson, farmer and lawyer, married her.

modern engineering work states that a horse can draw on the worst earthen road three times as much as he can carry on his back; on a good macadamised road, nine times as much ; on a plank road well laid and in good ordhr, twenty-five times as much ; on a smooth stone pavement, thirty-three times as much ; and on a steel railroad, fifty-four times this weight.

The Queen is a stern disciplinarian 'when occasion demands, as the Prince of Wales once found out to his cost. Her Majesty has always had a strong objection to tobacco smoke, and will not allow smoking to be in- dulged in in any rooms of the palaces used by her, tier, indeed, in any place where she is likely to smell tobacco. Even the late Prince Concert, forebore to smoke in her presence. On one occasion, however, she happened to smell the perfume of tobacco when in Buckingham Palace, and on milting an investigation, discovered that the young Prince of Wales (then in his minority) was the delinquent. Her Majesty forthwith gave orders that the culprit should he confined to his rooms for a month, and the order was rigorously carried out. The Prince, appar- ently has never forgotten that month's incarceration,for he never smokes in his royal mother's vicinity to this day.

One pleasing feature of the Queen's busy life is says the "Daily Chronicle" the keeping up, in her own hand, of a correspondence with all her children and grand-children, besides many other relatives and dear friends, such as, in the latter case, the ex-Empress Eugenie. This correspondencl is particularly large when she is, as at present, in retirement in her Highland home. Perhaps the most interesting letter her Majesty ever penned was that written to the Prince of Wales many years ago announcing his emancipation from par- ental authority and control, when, after warning him against flatterers, it is stated that she would never in- trude any advice upon him, although she would ever be ready to give it whenever he should think fit to seek it.

It is worthy of notice that,'almost without exception, the reign of every Queen in modern history has been at- tended by a wonderful advancement in the prosperity of her country. During the reign of Catherine the Great of Russia,for instance,the Northern Empire extended in almost every direction, and many social improvements took place during the reign. Then the reign of Queen Maria Theresa of Austria was distinguished by a period of prosperity hitherto unequalled, in spite of the Seven Years' War and political disturbances ; while it may be asserted without much fear of contradiction, that the reins of Queen " Bess " and Anne are unexampled-- with the exception of the present reign, ef course—in British history. In each reign the omnipotence of the United Kingdom was raised to a pitch that had never before been equalled. Even the unfortl/nate reign of Mary Queen of Scots resulted in some progress.

Fifty-six persons have been summoned at Carrickbeg for being concerned in a dog fight. Sergeant Kenny de- posed that early on the morning of the 17th Augnst he and Constable Daly in plain clothes went to Garrick railway station, where,with a crowd of people they got into a train. They went down to Fiddown, and from there to Brownswood,where witness saw about 200 peo- ple form a ring in the centre of a field. Two dogs were set fighting. After about an hour's fight the dogs were drawn and were given restoratives. The animals were then set fighting again. Restoratives were applied again. After ten minutes more the dogs were drawn, both being in a pitiable condition. Mr. Sullivan,for the defence, submitted there was no proof of cruelty. He admitted the parties were there,and the fight took place, but said it could not be construed into a case of cruelty in his client's case.—Two of the principals were fined £1 each,*and three others 10s. each, The other fifty were charged with aiding and abetting. One was convieted. and the other cases were adjourned for a writ certiorari to squash the conviction.

LOCAL ODD FELLOWSHIP.

PRINCE OF WALES LODGE.

The Prince of Wales Lodge of Oddfellows (Manchester Unity) having recently decided to remove to the Pelican Inn. Buttermarket-st., held their first meeting there on Saturday. The occasion was taken advantage of to pre- sent P.G. H tigh, their late treasurer, with a Past Grand Jewel,supplied by Messre.W.Battv and Sons,Manchester, and suitably engraved by Mr.J. T. Smith .Warrington,ae an appreciation of services rendered towards the lodge for the period of sevEn years as treasurer. There was a large number of members and visiting brethren present, amongst whom were Prov. GM. Whittle, Prov. D.G.M. Ryan,Prov.C.E.Emscn. The meeting was presided over by N.G. Hamblett, who spoke in high terms of the ser- vices rendered by P.G. Haigh,and on behalf of the lodge desired Prov. C.S. Emson to make the presentation, He did so in a very efficient manner,speaking at length upon the desirability of their having werkers of the stamp of the recipient in furthering the cause of Oddfellowship. He hoped the acknowledgment now bestowed upon him would be received in the same spirit as it was given, and act as a stimulant for Bro. Haigh to prove as useful a member in the future as he had done in the past. (Ap- plauss.)—P.G Haigh, in well chosen words, responded, and explained that now he had resigned office it was not his intention to be idle in their great cause of thrift and self dependence, but still to ge on and do what he could for the benefit of his lodge and district. (Applause.) The ordinary business was then proceeded with, including the initiation of two new members,after which the even- ing was spent in a social manner. Several songs and toasts having been given.and a hearty vote of thanks to the district officers for their attendance, and to P.G. Hamblett for presiding brought a very pleasant evening to a close. WEEKLY REPORT ON THE HEALTH OF

WARRINGTON.

During last week, which was the 37th, of the current year, the following births and deaths were registered : Births.

Deaths. 11

Warrington 35 Latchford

Total 37

The excess of deaths over birtha was 26. The death rate from all causes was equal to 9.8 per 1,000, against 176 the average of the previous four weeks, and against 382 the corresponding week of last year. The death rate from the seven chief zynaotic diseases was equal to 1.8 per 1,000, against 4•4 the average rate of the four previous weeks,and against 18.2 the corresponding week of 1895. The ages of mortality were- 0— 1 1-5 5-15 15-25 25-65 65 up. 3 2 6 11

The deaths included-1 from whooping cough, 1 from measles, 3 from heart-disease, 3 from brairi disease, I from hanging (suicide) 1 from t.rain injuries (murder). The folliwing cases of infectious disease have been reported during the week :—Erysipelas, 1 case, scarlet fever, 2 casas.

_ Total number of patients in hospital on 'Sept. 12th.-8 Meteor°lozy.—The mean temperature was 58 2 Fah., and therefore about the same as the precedine week. The maximum temp-rarure 70F., was recorded on the 11th inst., and the minimum temperature 45F., en the 7th inst.

The Rainfall measured •97 inch. The general direc- tinu was S.E., the reading of the Anemometer denoting 985 inilea.

JOHN H. GORNALL, Medical Officer of Health.

On Saturday night a drunken navvy. named Leech; entered a shop in Thorpe-road, Newton Heath, and was abusive to the person In charge. A vantnan who hap- prned to be delivering bread remonstrated with the fellow and being apprehensive of violence gave him a push! Leech fell backward, his head striking the ground. He was removed to the Infirmary in an unconscious state, and died shortly after his Omission. The cause of death was found to be effusion of blood on the brainithe result of the fall. A coroner's jury on Monday returned a verdict to that effect, and expressed the opinion that the push was given in self defence.

The Destruction THERE has during the Present of the week been no abatement of the Armenians. agitation on behalf of the Per- secuted Armenians. It has now been placed beyond doubt that the people of this country have heel, thoroughly aroused by the repeated crimes of the Turkish SULTAN. By the end of this week there will be few towns of any imporfr ance in England in which an indignation meet- , ing of one kind or another has not been held. The agitation has so far been almost unique in its character, seeing that although it deals with an important political question party spirit hag been entirely eliminated. At the commencemen of the movement some Conservatives deprecated even if they did not condemn the holding of pub' I ne lie meetings in denunciation of ABDUL RAMO' But their suspicions have now been allayed' There has been no attempt made by Liberals to snatch any party advantage from the feel- ings of abhorrence aroused by the massy cres. T:ie difficulties which confront the Government have been recognised by evel responsible speaker, and no attempt has bees made to force Lord SALISBURY into a course of action which is not. approved by his own judg- ment. The object in view is really to strengthen the Government in its dealings with the Sultan or any other foreign authority and not to weakeo it. The meetings that are being held are doing ° very useful work by impressing upon foreign governments the fact that Lord SALISBURY backed by a united nation in his efforts to bring about a reformation in Turkey. Unfortunately it. cannot be said that much improvement has 30:4 been effected. Even during the present weell there have been more massacres though on a cool' paratively small scale compared with those which, have been previously perpetrated. Six hundre° 1 more Armenians have, however, been added to the list of the slain in the Asiatic provinces of TurkeY) and there are many ominous indications the° still darker tragedies may soon follovi. In Constantinople the condition of el. surviving Armenians is pitiable. They ere' 1 harried and harassed in ienumerable wgge. by the SULTAN'S police, and in the meantiel il the fanaticism of the Moslem inhabitants is being further excited by tales of pretended discoverinS of bombs and dynamite in Armenian houses. o,ll° correspondent writing from the Turkish capit°4 says "you cannot conceive of the harrowing offer ing which is entailed by this Reign of Terror. IT- prisonment, torture, death, form but a part Of. zoti- Think of houses entered at dead of night, t_., I, fathers and sons and brothers being carried Ofr VJ the police, and of the women and children left . rrhil3P an agony of uncertainty as to their fate. what it it means to those leit at home, when tbe business man or the cierk, plucking up some little. rrbeg courage, leaves for the city in the morning. all know that he may be imprisoned before as° upon some groundless charges. So the days in misery and as for the nights, there are th° OOP sands who cannot sleep. 'They tear each foo se. 'is the tread of an invader of their Wit", The massacres were horribly brutal, .0 continued persecution is mercilessly cruel, but 11 hundreds of houses there are men and women la children being tor:nred to death by fear, the anxiety and despair. We cannot reckon UP d broken hearts as we can count up the dea3 B_lloa missing, but their number is awfully large, :or their pain is unspeakable." The " bitter cry . this correspondent is in harmony with al/ tu

HOLY TRINITY CHURCH, RUNCORN..

The harvest thanksgiving services at this church were continued on Sunday, when two sermons were preached by the Rev. E. Jobsen,vicar of Brierfield. Special music was ably rendered by the choir. The offertories were in aid of the curates' fund. There were large congrega- tions.

Sr. MICHAEL'S CHURCH RUNCORN.

2' On Thursday evening, the annual services of thanks- giving for the harvest, were commenced in the above church. The sacred edifice was tastefully adorned. There was a large congregation, and the Rev. C. C. Eleum M.A., vicar of St. Agnes, Liverpool preached an appropriate sermon. TUE EVENING CLASSES.

Norman, J.P., will distribute the prizes and certificates rained last year by tke successful scholars. All interested in Education are invited to attend. Nsw THEATRE FOR RUNCORN.

A site for the erection of the new theatre for Run- corn, has been obtained in Lowland's-road opposite Run- corn Station (L. & N.W.) ' Plans have been prepared by W. Davies, arehitect, Devonshire Buildings, Runcorn ; and have been approved by the promoter Mr. Csddick. The construction will be of brick and stone. The total estimated cost is about £3OOO and the theatre is expected to accommodate 1,500 persons. The work which is to be put in hand at once, is expected to be completed be- fore the end of the year. ST. PAUL'S WESLEYAN CHURCH. RUNCORN.

The above church was crowded on Sunday, the occas- ion being the annual harvest festival. The sacred edifice was beautifully embellished by a number of willing workers. The choir under the leadership of Mr. Morris, rendered special music, the evening anthems being" Ye shall dwell in the Land" and " Praise the Lord." The preachers for the day were, morning, the Rev. W. George; evening. the Rev. T. Brackenbury. The ser- vices were continued on Monday,when the preacher was the Rev. R. G. Clifton. The collections were on behalf of the circuit funds, in connection with which it was stated there was a considerable debt, owing to heavy expenses.

A BRLYE RUNCORN VOLUNTEidt.

The War Office authorities have awarded Lance Corporal William Horton, G Company, of the Runcorn detachment Earl of Chester's Rfles, the honorary testi- monial of the Royal Humane Saciety, for saving the life of Private Belie, of the Royal Welsh Fusiliers, whilst encamped in Towyn,in July last. It will be remembered that Corporal Horton saw Private Belie struggling in the water close to the camp. Horton jumped in(in uniform) without a moment's hesitation, and rescued the man. The young private was insensible when brought on land and Corporal Horten,who has passed a course of lectures under Dr. McDougall for the St. John ambulance, ren- dered valuable assistance in bringing the Welsh Fusilier round.

UNITED lIETBODIST FEES OKI:TECH.

The Rev. J. Stafford preached a sermon on Sunday in the United Methodist Free Church, Runcorn, on the Armenian Qaestion. The rev. eentlennan, in the coiirse of his remarks,strongly condemned the manner in which the Christians of Turkey are being dealt with,and urged that the time had arrived when such cruelties should be stopped. At the close of the rev. gentleman's remarks, the following resolution was submitted :—" We express our unqualified abhorrence of the outrages and mass- acres of the Armenian Christians by the Sultan of Turkey, and earnestly appeal to Lord Salisbury (Prime Minister) to adopt, with all haste,means,with or without the aid of other powers,that will remove them„and cause better rule in Turkey." The preposition was unani- mously carried. There was a large congregation.

THE RUNCORN MUSICAL SOCIETY.

ANNUAL MEETING.

The annual meeting of the above Society was held on Tuesday evening in the Bethesda Lecture Hall,Runcorn, Mr.J. Brundrit occupying the chair. The Secretary,Mr. W. R.Pierpoint, presented his renort,which showed that the past season, which was the eighth of the Society's working, was one of unqualified success from a musical point of view. They retained the patronage of his Grace the Duke of Westminster, and Mr. Bruno rit had been their president for the eighth time, their vice presidents numbered 86, and the singing members 75. The usual three concerts had been held, the first on °etcher 29th, 1895, which was of a miscellaneous character,but failed to draw,as was anticipated. The second concert took place on December 16th, 1895, when they presented Barnett's " Ancient Mariner " ; whilst the third concert was held on March the 26th, 1896, when "Elijah was produced. the concerts were not as well attended as they might have been. Mr. Booth, the treasurer, submitted the balance sheet which snowed that the season commenced with an ad- verse balance of 16s. Bs. The receipts at the first con- cert were, £47 43.11 d. and the expenditure,£sB 13s. 7d.; second concert. receipts, £4O ss, 3d.•, expenditure. £56 17s. 3d.; third concert, income, £46 17s. 10d.; expendi- ture, £EI.3 Is. I.ld. The summary showed accounts to be due, amounting to £64 3s. 4d., towards which they had £l9 19 in hand, leaving a net loss on the workings of the season of £44 4s. 441. The Chairman stated that the estimated cost for another season's concerts would he £136 25., a consider- able sum less than what they expended the last year. He moved the adoption of the reports.—Mr. Crosby seconded.—Mr.Brundrit said he would like to hear some of tho opinions of the members present as to the future of the Society. They had to decide whether it should be con- tinued or not.—Mr. Scott said, he noticed that they bad the presence ef some of the vicP-presidents,and he should like to have their opinions on the inatter.—One of the vice-presidents who was amongst the audience stood up and said the committee had well threshed the matter out before.in trying to devise some way as to the future. They were pretty nearly divided on the subject, and so it was left over until that night, to get a fair expression. But to begin with, he though that the meeting was a very poorly attended one. The ladies had turned up very well, but the men had not. To begin' another season with some £44 around their necks, was rather a difficult matter.—Another member expressed his views on the subject and said, the best thing to do in plain words was to "Jack up."—The Chairman suggested that they should adjourn the meeting in order to give them an opportunity of talking the matter over.—lt was ultimately decided that the Society should continue for another year.

The election of officers then took place. Mr. Brundrit was re-elected president, and the vice-presidents were also re-elected. Mr. Scott Wag elected secretary, with Mr. Fiendley as assistant, Mr. It. W. Piervint,treasurer; Mr. Marrow, librarian ; Mr. Musgrove, organist and accompanist. A committee of six gentlemen and ladies was also elected.

It was stated that at the first concert, which is to be held on the 17th December, the " Messiah" would be rendered.

SHOPBREAKING.—At the Runcorn Pollee Court on Thursday afternoon, before Mr. F. J. Collier's shop, said he left the premises secured on Wednesday at noon. When he went to open next morning, he found the fanlight broken. He bad:never seen the prissners before.—P.C. Bancroft said his attention was called to the shop about midnight by the breaking of glass. He listened at Ow front door for some time, avid hard a noise inside the shop. He went round to the back of the premises, and there saw Timperley looking over the wall. The latter on seeing him ran off. Witness followed him and Pimb- lett. The other prisoner got away, but, was apprehended later in the day by P.C. Gregorv.—The prisoners were remanded for a week for el qoiries to be made.

THE EXA MINER SAT UR Y. SEPTEMBER 26 1896.

RUNCORN SCHOOL BOARD.

A special meeting of this Board was held is the Board Room on Tuesday evening. There were present, Messrs. J. Pritchard (chairman), F. J. Norman, E. Imisou,Canou Wood, the Rev. Dr. Gastaldi and Mr. Moss, clerk.

ELECTION OF • NEW MEMBER.

The Chairman said their first business was to elect a new member in the place of Dr. McDougall resigned.— Canon Wood said he had a name to submit, which he thought would be received with satisfaction by the Board. As it fell to the duty of the Board to elect a new member he had pleasure in proposing Mr. Charles Timmins. He was well-known to them all, as a man who has taken considerable interest in matters of ele- mentary education. Mr. Timmins represented a firm of considerable importance in the town, so that in every way he was a suitable candidate. Be (Canon Wood) also proposed a vote of sympathy with their late colleague Hr. McDougall in his illness.—Mr. Imison seconded the proposities.—Dr. Gastaldi said Mr. Tim- mins would be a very efficient member.—The Chairman put the proposition to the meeting and it was unani- mously carried.

HEA.TY SENTENCE ON A RUNCORN MOTHER.

EXPOSING HER CHILD.

• On Monday at the Runcorn Petty Sessions, Ellen Moran, a well-known character, was charged with being drunk and disorderly in Bridge street, on Saturday night and also with exposing her child, John William Moran, in such a manner, as likely to cause him unnecessary suffering. P.C. Gregory said, that about 11.15 p.m. on Saturday he was standing near the Police Station. The prisoner came to him, in a very drunken state, and said she wanted lodgings. She was 'tarrying the child at the time. Prisoner fell on the steps and knocked the child's head. Witness examined the child, and found all its clething wet. A Miss Cooper was sent for; and she put fresh clothes on the little child ; Witnes4 often saw her with the child, (when it was raining) sitting in the streets. Prisoner only came out of prison on Friday, and was drunk the same night. The child who was only 8 months old, had spent almost the whole of his life in prison. On the suggestion of the magistrates Superintendent Okell withdrew the charge of drunk and disorderly. The Bench addressing the prisoner said, "you are one of those people who give the country a lot of trouble. With the hope of a longer sentence which we have power to give, with such labour as you are able to perform,we hope this is the last visit you will make to Runcorn." She was sentenced to .ix months' imprisonment. The prisoner on hearing the decision of the magis- trates began to cry and had to be forcibly taken out of the court.

Before Messrs. J. Brundrit (chairman), J. Handley, P. Speakman, S. Taylor, F. J. Norman and J. W. Lightburn.

===== 0002601_18960926_art0057.txt | included=true | included =====
AN OFF BEER LICENSE REFUSED.

Mr. Arthur Browne. solicitor, Warrington,said he was instructed to apply for two separate licenses one an out door beerhouee,and the other a wine lioense on behalf of George Partington,grocer,Balfour-street, Runcorn.—Mr. Browne continuing said their worships would remember the same application was made by him a month age ; but he came on different merits than before and to offer certain facilities. He was prepared to give up certain houses if their worships granted the off beer license. On the last occasion whilst' the bench were privately con- sidering their decision, Superintendent Okell suggested that Messrs. Greenell. Whitley, and Co., of Wilderspool Brewery,Warrington,should purchase the house and give up licenses of other places in the eentre of the town, where; there were so many. The suggestion of the superintendent was carried to the officials of the Brewery Company, with the result that the com- pany had agreed to buy the premises, conditionally to the licence being granted, and to give up the licences of other houses in the centre of the town. The district in which Mr. Partington's shop was situated was in Higher R ncorn and was a residential portion. There were 476 voters in the ward, which only contained one fully licensed house. The name of the house was the Traveller's Rest. If the Bench granted the licence for beer, they were bound to grant the wine licence, if the necessary qualifications were proved. Mr. Browne pointed out that a number of respectable workingmen, residing in the locality were unable to obtain beer with- out either going themselves or sending their wives and children to the public house some 400 yards away. William Partington said, he occupied the premises in Balfour-road, his wife was the owner. Since the last application, he had entered into an agreement for the sale of the house to Messss. Greenall and Co. The only beerhouee was the Traveller's Rest. There were in the ward 476 voters on the parochial list. Witness produced a memorial in favour of the application containing 182 signatures. He personally saw the greater portion of them signed. Peter Radford,Lightburn-street, fitters' labourer, said his house was over 400 yards away from a public inn. It was not possible for him to get a glass of beer in his dinner hour if he wanted one. He had no interest what- ever in the application. Witness went round and helped to get the signatures on the memorial. They had (out of the number of people they went to) only six refusals. _

William Lewis,Chaucer-st., George Baker, Lightburn- street, and James Sadler, Balfour-street, spoke in favour of the application. Mr. William Sutton, assistant overseer produced the rate book and said the assessment of the house was £25 gross ; and £22 net. Mr. Browne mentioned the licenses it was proposed to give up (providing' the present application be granted). The were viz.: The Three Crowns beerhouse in Cooper- at. and the Rose and Shamrock, Water-st. Superintendent Okell on behalf of the police, said there was no objection on the conditions promised. Mr. Browne said they would give such an undertak- ing. The magistrates then retired, and on returning into court Mr. Brundrit said they had given the appli- cations their most c treful consideration and bad come to the conclusion to refuse the beer license. The wine license was granted. A TRANSFSR.—AIbert Griffith applied for the transfer of ti:e Oddfellows Arms, ihellingtm-st. The application was granted.

===== 0002601_18960926_art0058.txt | included=true | included =====
PEAItSON AND KNOWLES COAL AND IRON COMPANY LIMITED.

The report of the directors of this company for the past year was issued on Saturday. The directors say:— The profits for the year ending June 30, 1896, amount to £18.320 15s. Bd., aad the balance brought torward from last )par is £R,562 103. 6d., together £26,883 6s. 2d. lire direetora rcummend that the preference dividend at the rite oil 6 per cent per annum be paid on the A shares up to December 31, 1895, but the present stateof the accaunts does not, in their opinion, warrant any farther distribution. The directors also recommend thAt £5,000 be added to the depreciation fund, and that t',e balance, £9,256 14s. 2d., be carried forward to the next account. It has been found necessary to make considerable additions to the machinery and appliances tor dealing with the coal on the surface at the lace collieries, and modern screens, elevators, &c., have been erected at most of the pits. The expenditure on this aceeunt falling within the year has amounted to £6.159 84. 9d, Important alterations are also in progress at rile ironworks, but being incomplete these are only pfrtially dealt with in tbe present account. To put the c. mpany into a better position to meet the continually increasing cmpetition in both home and foreign nett kets,the directors are of opinion that it will be neces- sary to incur at an early date further expenuitnre at both i collieries and ironworks, and they have uuder consideration the question of raising the requisite funds by the issue of debentures, due notice of which will be given. Au improved demand in some department of the iron trade has enabled the directors to restart a portion ut till Wigan works, the stoppage of which was referred oin the last report. The position of the coal trade during the whole of the past year has been most unsatis- iactory. Both prices and demand have constantly declined, so that the working of this departmeWl has lava altogether unprofitable,and the prospect at present glocanyland uncertain. The iron, steel, and engineer- ing trades have benefited to some extent by the better commercial position of the country generally, but the ) ear's trade has been carried on under great ;difficulties.

LOCAL HARVEST FESTIVALS.

CAIRO•STEET CHAPEL, WARRING TON.

On Sunday, the annual harvest thanksgiving services in connection with the above place of worship were held and the pulpit was occupied morning and evening by the pastor, the Rev. W. H. Drummond. The interior of the building was skilfully and testetully decorated for the occasion, and presented a very attractive appearance. The treatment of the pulpit was particularly effective. The musical portion of the services was in every way commendable. Mr. James Muir was at the organ, and in the morning the choir nave an excellent rendering of the anthem, " Lord how mindful," while that selected for the evening was " While the earth remaineth." Appropriate harvest hymns were also sung. It may be interesting to note that the new form of service was used for the first time on Sunday. Mr. Drummoad explained that many ef the prayers were those that had been used in the ancient church, and in the early English Church, and that they had been handed down from generation to generation, and were such as could be used in that chapel, seeing that they had been a help to Christians of all ages. The prayers in the fourth service had been compiled by Dr. Martineau. The preacher, in the even- ing, took for his text the words, " Lift up your eyes, and look on the fields: for they are white already to harvest," St. John iv. 35. He showed the different ways in which men viewed the world, and also the man- ner in which Christ viewed it. He was always going forth giving Himself to ethers in love and sympathy,and it was that quality which He always tried to inspire ia His disciples. He wou'd teach them to look at the world through His eyes, to view it as He viewed it ; to use its opportunities as He used them. To Him there was a divine call to duty OR every side, and to be a Christian was to share the spirit of Jesus Christ. To share His spirit was to have a life open to all the finer opportuni- ties of the world—to go forth into the world and reap the harvest fields. And life was full of these invitations of God. Allusion was made to the work of the scholar, the reformer, and the scientific observer, and Mr. Drum- mlnd observed that no one could look forth upon this world, and come into contact with human life, withetit being conscious of the great seed there was of His life to spend itself ia doing good to others. That night they might say that they entered upon their winter's work in that church after the long summer days. They entered upon it with new opportunities, with wider possibiliqes of usefulness, and all around them there were seen, women, and children who needed the work and influence which they could bring to bear upon them. To theta in that place there was coming the divine call to gl forth into the harvest fields which were already white, and waiting for them. BOLD-STREET WESLEYA.N CHURCH, WARRINGTON. - - -

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

RUGBY FOOT/SALL.

It was a pity for the Wiganites who opened thcir ground on Saturday that they had such an unfavour- able day. The day was wretched throughout ; there was heavy thunder, with rain coining down in torrents. It could rightly be said, tha it was no day to play foot- ball.

The Runcornites rsached Wigan in eood time, with their usual team,and notwithstanding the heavy showers the teams took up their positions on the field. When the ball was set rolling, the visitors showed a marked improvement, in their forward play. Robinson was several times conspicuous by his mode of tackling, whilst Butterworth in grand style, marked the home centres. There was nothing scored when half-time was called. The sodden condition of the ground and the, slippery nature of the ball was no doubt the cause of

In the second half, Runcorn began tq work well to- gether. About twenty minutes irom the restart Butter- worth broke away, seorod a splendid try, and so put the visitors in a leading posicion. Be was loudly cheered by the spectators, for the einart way in which he got possession of the leather, and shot away with the home men at his beets.

EXTRAORDINARY LOCAL CLERICAL k`,•CANDAL.

SERIOUS ALLEGATIONS AGAINST CANON DODD.

At a special meeting of the Chester Consistory Court on Wednesday in the Refectory, Chester Cathedral, Lhancellor Espin sat, with Revs. Canons NV. Symonds (Stockport), and Hignett, Rev. and Hon. C. F. Croes,and Messrs. Duncan Graham and C S. Rouudell as assessors, to hear complaints brought under the Clergy Discipline Act, 1892, against the Rev. Canon Dodd, vicar of Stret- ton, near Warrington, for the last twenty-eight years. The allegations were that on the 4th May last he was guilty of immoral conduct by speaking ribald or indecent language to Amy Dooley, wife of James Dooley, Pepper- ste,Appletom, near Warrington ; that between the Ist January and May 4th he was guilty of immoral conduct, acts and habit, with Annie Jones, a servant girl at the vicarage, of Appleton Cross; and that between the 3rd end 13th May he was guilty of immoral acts and conduct by kissing Sarah Perrin, another servant girl at the vicarage, of Salisbury- street, Warrington. The Bishop of Chester was the preeecator,and Mr. E. N. Lloyd, barrister, appeared for him, instructed by Mr. E. S. Giles. The defendant was represented by Mr. F H. Mellor, barrister, London, in- structed by Messrs. Greene!! and Buckton, Warrington. Mr. Lloyd havine opened the case, Amy Dooley stated that shortly after she was married by the defendant he called upon her, closed the doer be- hind him, and attempted te kiss her, saying " Good morning, Amy." He put his arm round her, and said that as vicar he was entitled to a kiss. She complained at the vicarage next day.—Mary Lee,Appleton, deposed to receiving a eomplaint from Mrs. Dooley about the conduct of the vicar.—Mrs. Cooper (Mrs. Dooley's mother) stated that she was present at the interview at the vicarage when her daughter complained to Mrs. Dodd about having been insulted. Canon Dodd denied having insulted her, but said if she had taken what had been said as an insult he would apologise. He denied having made certain suggestions about her daughter.— Thomas Doeley,complainent's husband,in cross-examin- ation, refused to say whether a question arose at his marriage as to his real name. The second case was then gone into. Annie Jonee said she was sixteen last April. She entered defendant's service last December, and in about a week Canon Dodd took her on his knee and kissed her. A week later,when in his study, he again took her on his knee and behaved improperly towards her. 'This happened several times, ' Once he tried to put her on his bed in his dressing room. She did not know what the date was. He gave her E.,. once and two half-crowns afterwards. After she had left his employ he broueht to her home her month's salary, although she had only worked a fortnight. He also gave her 2s. 6,1. after he had b-haved improperly to her. She was dismissed on a charge of having stolen several articles which were found in her roem.—Cross-exam- ined Witness did not remember that she was threatened witn disnsissal the first week she was at the vicarage for dishonesty and lying, or that the Canon interceded as , she was young and she was kept on. She never made a coraplaint to anyone except to Mrs. Seiratt, a char- ' woman. She saw Dr. Moberley at her mother's house, and the question was then raised for the first time as to the Cason's conduct.—Elizabeth Spratt depesed to receiving the complaint.—The gin's mother and grand- mother gave evidence, the latter stating that she first heard of the affair through rumour in the village. The third case was that brought by Sarah Perrin, a. servant who fellowed Jones, and who stated that defen- dant kissed her three times in the first fortnight. One morning he kissed her on the back of the neck and said, " You have pretty hair and soft cheeks, and are a very nice girl." She told him she did not want him to do that. When she was putting the Canon's slippers on he pulled the back of her dress. She pulled the dress out oh' his hand, and next morning she left the house, telling her mistress that she could not stay any longer in con- sequence of the Canon's conduct. Mr. Mellor, addressing the court for the defence, said that he knew of no case that was so difficult to deal with as line of that description. He pointed out that Canon Dodd had lived with his family many years at Stretton. It seemed to him to be an extraordinary thine ef a mau of the defendant's experience, as was alleged, should have been such a fool as to give himself away by assaulting girls. Referring to the charge made by Mrs. Doeley, he said the Canon had officiated at her wedding and subsequently in the vestry questioued her husband respecting his name. Her story was that Cannon Dodd at ten o'clock in the moraine went straight to her house, and seeing her said " G-oect mernine, Amy ; " and then having closed the door committed the assault upon her. Now, although perhaps some words uttered seemed to be ' unfortunate, they were very natural and ordinary, and he (Mr. Mellor) felt that the eourt would give his client the benefit of a pure mind. A De. Moberley, who had interested himself in the case, had raked up these charges, and had actually written to the Bishop about the time when he (ler. Moberly) was visiting the canon's house partaking of his hospitality and enjoying lawn tennis. He felt sure that when the court sifted the whole •f the case it woeld be seen that it had been get up through Dr. Meberley. The canon denied ever as- saulting Mrs. Dooley or using °Nemo language to her. In the case of Annie Jones, he did not believe that the court would believe anythicg she had said. If a story told by a person was to be believed the conduct of that person should be above reproach, but after the girl had been in Mrs. Dodd'e serviee a week she was detected stealing some articles. She was accused of the thefts by Mrs. D.xid, who said she should leave in coneequence of it. But the canon interceded for the girl, and per- auaded his wife to give her another trial. The girl's story was that from that very day until she left her ser- vice the canon assaulted her in various wave. There were, however, no dates given, and flaking upon which the prosecution could rely. The canon also denied interfering with her indecently. She had not even complained to her mother. Counsel contended that the girl's story was unworthy of belief, and that it was sot supported by a tittle of evi- deac,e. Dealing with the third ease, he remarked that Sarah Perrin appeared to be a respectable eirl, but the most that she said and complained to her mother about was that Canon Dodd had kissed her on two occasions. He admitted to do Puce a thing as that was indiscreet. She was only fifteen or sixteen years old. Undoubtedly Canon Dodd had said all along that he did kiss her; bat he did not do anything approaching the slightest inde- eency. She had told Mrs. Dodd that she liked living at the vicarage,but the canon had kissed her and she didn't like it. It was said that the canon had taken hold of her dress, but he argued there was nothing indecent about that.

Chancellor Espin remarked that it was said the canon kissed the girl three times.

Mr. Mellor said that the kissing might be indiscreet and improper, but the canon was charged in the com- plaint with indecency. Kissing could not be said to be an act of indecency. Canon Dodd was then called. He stated he had been vicar of Stratton twenty-eight years, and had been for sixteen years a canon of Chester Cathedral. He had held other appointments including that of proctor at the Convocation of Yerk. The first he heard of any com- plaint aeainst himself was when Mrs. Dooley came to his house. He heard her complaint, denied committing any assault, and said that if he had done anything which might be considered wrong he was fully prepared to apologise. He remembered when she was married,and after questioning Mr. Dooley about his name, a person present gave him a different name. He visited Mrs. Dooley, and in the course of some conversation said "I suppose you knew the old Cheshire custom that all brides kiss the parson?" at which she laughed. He denied attempting to kiss her or assault her : but he asked her how her husband was getting on because he 'mew he was of intemperate habits. He denied using ribald or indecent language te her. He also gave a denial to the allegation made by the girl Jones, whilst he admitted having given Perrin " the most innocent kiss in the world." le cross-examination, witness said he could not say when it occurred to him that Mrs. Dooley had been act- ing with a wicked motive. When Mrs. Dooley came to the vicarage he honestly believed that she imagined that she had been insulted. The story told by Annie Janes was utterly and entirely false, and invented out of a spirit of revenge in consequence of her being discharged. She was both dishonest and untruthful while in his em- ploy. On the first occasion of her dishonesty he inter- ceded with Mrs.Dodd for her. 'lhat was the only occasion on which he kissed the girl, and then she was crying bitterly. Mr. Lloyd: Is it a common occurrence to kiss a do- mestic servant in this way P—Certainly not.—Then, in reference to Annie Jones, there was a special occasion for it ?—Yes.—And that was the occasion ot the girl be- ing discharged ?—Yes,l felt sorry for her ; she was cry- ing very bitterly,and as she was a mere child I patted her on the shoulder,and kissed her on the forehead. I did nothing else. Cross-examination continued.—When the girl was dis- charged, he did not pay her more than her wages. He thought a woman like Mrs. Dooley, who could have been under the impression that he had insulted her and used improper language to her, was capable of saying and inventing anything, and adhering to it for months after she invented it. He had no reason to think Sarah Perrin was not a respectable woman. Mrs. Dedd was then called, and related what took place in the vicarage when Mrs. Cooper and Mrs. Dooley came to make their complaint. After hearine what Mrs. Dooley had to say, Canon Dodd said, "Woman, what can you mean? You have put a perfectly wrong interpretation on what I said." He added afterward" "If you think I have been rude to you in any way I etn extremely sorry ; that is all I have to say." Canon Dodd did not stop them speaking, and they left of their own initiative. They made no complaint to witness of actual indecency. Witness then gave evidence as to the dishonesty and untruthfulness of 'Llie girl Annie Jones. The girl Annie Perrin complained to witness that Canon Dodd had kissed her, and said she did not approve of it, and would rather leave. She left the same day. She never complained te witness about Canon Dodd moving her dress, or anything of the kind. Further evidence of a similar nature tending to dis- credit the girl Jones's story having bean given, Mr. Mellor called witnesses to speak to the high clair- acter of Canon Dodd.

I After about half a dozen witnesses had given such ! testimony, The Chancellor interposed remarks that further evid- ence of the kind was superfluous. The court had no doubt about the character of Canon Dodd. His public position in the diocese, and the fact that he had been elected by his brethren time after time to represent them at Convocation, placed that beyond doubt. Mr. Mellor and Mr. Lloyd having delivered their 1 addresses, the court adjourned, and after a deliberation in private lasting about balf-an-hour, returned to the refectory.—The Chancellor said the curt had given very 1 .careful and conscientious attention to the evidence that had been laid before them, and it was with great pain 1 and reluctance that they felt it to be their unavoidable duty to declare that the whole of the three charges had been proved. It would be now for him to consider what recommendation he must offer to his lordship the bishop as to the proper method of dealing with this case, and he proposed to do so in the Consistory Court at two o'clock next day. JUDGMENT: SENTENCE DEFERRED.

On Thursday afternoon, at the conclusion of the or- dinary business of the Consistory Court, Chancellor Espin proceeded to read his judgment. Canon Dodd was pr.sent, and the attendance also included Mr. Greenall, his solicitor, and Mr. Giles. the Bishop's solici- tor.

The Chancellor read a long official report of the in- quiry, and said he had had the assistance of five pains- taking assessors—three of them brother clergymen in- the diocese and two of them experienced county magistrates. After carefully weighing the evidence and making due allowance for the fact that some of the witnesses for the prosecution and especially those under the second charge, were open to tne observation that it was necessary to take their evi- dence with caution, the Court was of opinion that each of the three charges had been preyed. The Act of 1892 had the sanction of the two Convocations, and therefore might be deemed to have ecclesiastical as well as statut- able standing. He should notify to the Bishop that the defendant had been found guilty and what sentence should be passed. The case was a very painful one, especially considering the long standing of the defendant in the dkcese. Under the Act special regard had to be had in such cases to the parish or place concerned, and not to precedents in the way of punishments. Sentence would be pronounced on a later date to be notified. Defendant must be condemned to pay the costs of the prosecution.

MR. GLADSTONE AND THE ARMENIANS.

GREAT SPEECH AF LIVERPOOL

Several thousand persons assembled at Hangler's Cir- cus, Liverpool, on Thursday afternoon te hear Mr. Glad- stone's speech on the Turkish atrocities, and the right hoe. gentleman on arriving at the meeting, accompanied by Mrs. Gladstone, was received with immense enthu- siasm. The Earl of Derby (Lord Mayor of the city) pre- sided. The following resolution was proposed by Mr. E. Lawrence, seconded by Mr. E. Ev.ins, jun.,supported by Mr. Warr (Conservative member for Eset Toxteth) and Sir E. R.Russell,and carried with acclamation :—" That this meeting desires to express its indignation and abhorrence of the cruel treatownt to .‘ hi ,:14 the Armen- ian Christians are beiog subjected by their Turkish rulers and the maseacres which have recently taken place at Constantinop:e, which are a disgrace to the civilisation of the nineteenth century." Mr. Gladstone, on rising, was again greeted with prolonged cheers. He said he made no claim to autturity in any shape; he came to that meeting as a loyal sub- ject of her Majesty, and as their fellow towns- man. They stood that day on a higher platform thab that of party,and he thought they would find,when in the course of time they were made more largely ac- quainted with the inner relations of Governments in the transactions of the last two years, that the present de- plorable situation, to whomsoever it might be due, was not due either to the act or default of the Government ,ot this great country. This was no crusade against Mahometanism ; it was no declaration of universal con- , demnation of the Mahometan and the Turkish Empire. After expressing the hope that it would not be too easily , assumed that in the attitude and tone aisumed by several 'of the Continental Governments those Govern- , ineuts were the faithful representatives of the , people whom they ruled, the right hon. gentleman said the distinction of the massacres of Constantinople as compared with those that had taken place before was not in their moral Manly. It was in this, that ti all the ether dreadful manifestations, which had formerly been displayed in the face of the world there was added con- summate insolence. Translate the acts of the Sulta n into words,end they become these :—" I have tried your patience in distant places ; I will try it under your eyes. I have desolated my provinces; I will now desolate my capital. I have found that your sensitiveness has nut been effectually provoked by all that I have hitherto done. I will come nearer to you, and ste whether by vicinity I shall or shall not awake the wrath which has slept so long." Some of it had been awakened. To what extent? The weakness of diplomacy, he trusted, was now about to be strength- ened by the echoes of a nation's voice. The Sultan had paraded massacre under the eyes of the representetives of every Court in Europe. He remained unpunished and intact, and boldly asserted his innocence and his merit. , Was not that exactly the position in which lie might expect that he would go forward? He (Mr. Gladstone) thought that usually the Concert of Europe bad failed in what was known as the Eastern Question, and that in the present instance the Powers collectively had undergone miserable disgrace. He did not partition that disgrace. He had expressed his strong hope that it would be found that our Government was not the party that had brought such a result upon us. But we had arrived at a point at which it had become neces- sary to strengthen the bands of the Executive Government by an expression of the national will. In his opinion the presence of the six Powers at Constantinople and of the ambas- sadors of the Sultan in the Turkish capital during those massacres had worked as a dis ilia countenance and support of the Sultan, and had carried to the general mind of Europe the belief that the differences between them and the Sultan were matters to be arranged by a few amicable exchanges of courtesies and argument. It was in these circumstances that the citizens of Liver- pool were asked to pass the resolution which he now I moved :—That this meeting trusts her Majesty's Ministers, realising to the fullest extent the terrible condition in which their fellow- Christians are placed, will do everything pos- sible to obtain for them full security and protection,and this meeting assures her Majesty's. Ministers that they may rely on the cordial support of the citizens of Liverpool in whatever steps they may feel it necessary to take for that purpose.' Although the resolution did not express it, yet it was not the intention of the meet- ing, he thought, to express a desire that everything that in the nature of things was abstractly possible should be done. The rules of prudence must be observed, and the rules ef prudence, he thought, neither required nor permitted, nor did duty, in his opinion, either require or permit, that we, for the sake of the great object we had in view, should place ourselves in a condition of war with united Europe, or should take measuies which would plunge Europe generally into a irate of war. But while he fully admitted and recog- nised that the possible measure need not include a policy which gave just cause of complaint to the Powers of Europe, he old not admit that we ought to forego our own convictions or duty in taking measures that we might be said to feel opporune. Whilst he admitted that it was of the utmost importance that we should study every means ef consulting the sentiment of cthpr Powers and of carrying them along with us, he did not believe that the way to carry them along with us was to show a servile let:lenination under all circumstances, and whatever they might decide, to make their conscience beforehand the measure of our own. We were entitled to threaten Turkey with coercion, which, judiciously applied, had eften been the means of averting war. The first thing to do would be to require Turkey to fulfil her treaty obligations. If that step failed, the next step •should he to recall the Briti-h Anib4ssador from Constantinople and to dismiss the turkish Ambas- sador from London. He should then propose that, on this suspension of diplomatic relations, England should inform the Sultan that she intended to take into con- sideration the means of enforcing her just, legal, and humane demands. 'll.is should be ace. mpanied by declaration that on no account woul I we turn a war to our own private and particular advantage, and if things came to th's point he did not be- lieve that any part ot Europe would make war to ensure a continuance of inesQacres more terrible than had ever been recorded in the dismal and deplorable history of human crime. The right bon. gentleman spoke for an hour and twenty minutes, and when he sat down the resolution he proposed was seconded and passed amid cheers.

GENERAL NEWS.

In important case, brought forward by the Inland Revenue, was heard at Darlington Bllice Court on Mon- daN . Alfred Lord, a canvasser for a sewing machine company, who travelled the district around Darlington in a conveyance, wa% summoned for hawking without a license. 11-. was admitted that he disposed of machines, but the contention was that he came under ettemption frun the Hawker's Act as a commercial traveller far the manufacture of the maciiinea. After hearing argu- ments, the Bench fined Lord £2 for hawking without a license. Mr. Wansbrough,of London, who appeared for the company,stated that that firm alone had over 4,000 men similarly employed in various parts. He asked for a case tor a higher curt, which was granted.

XA.JOR. W. H. WALKER'S APPROACH-

ING MARRIAGE.

The inhabitants of the village of Geteacre have ;wailed themselves of the opportunity afforded by the approaeleine, marriage of Major W. Hall Walker to pay him a grace"ful compliment, and to express by a hand- some gift their esteem and goodwill. The gift chosen by the committee having charge of the matter is in the form of very handsome clock and ornaments in real broeze, richly gilt. Below the dial of the clock is a panel illustrative of one of the crusade expeditions in the eleventh and twelfth centuries to retake Palestine from the Mussultaans. This scene is artistically worked out in oxydised and frosted silver and is a beautiful example cf bas relief. The accompanying vases have also sub- jects representing episodes of the battles of Jerusalem, taken from piceures at Versailles. At the base r, f the clock is an elaborate silver gilt shield bearing the crest and motto of the recipient, and also the following in- scription :—" Presented to Major Hall Walker by the in- habitants of Gateecre on the oceesion of his marriage, September 29th.1896." The cloek and vases were sup- plied by Mr. R. C. Oldfield, of Old Post Office-place. Liverpool. A writer in the " World" hear that Miss Sophie Sheridan's wedding on the 29`,h will be the pretti- est affair. Who the bridesmaids will be Ido rfot know for certain, as several alterations have been made ; but it was to be a sine qua non that all should be good-look- jog. The names of Miss Elaine Guest and Miss Rea Ken- nard were first mentioned ; but now the sight will in- clude Mims Hilda Paget (who was so reach admired as Galatea at the theatrical.; at Canford). Miss rp,Ftpr gen- nerd, Mrs. Arthur Kennard's daughter ; Miss Chetworle, Lady Chetwod.'s daughter ; the bridegroom's sister, Miss Lisette Walker; and Miss Thynne; while the (laugh" tars of Mrs. Bammfylde and Mrs. Court will carry tho train. Because it is on Michaelmas Day the bridesmaids will all be presented with diamond eeese brooches, and the best man, Sir Peter Walker. will be presented with a diamond geese as pin. Mr. William Walker,the bride- groom, is, I am told, extremely wealthy, and holds con- siderable property in Lancashireoe well as ruing yaeths and racing horses. His pretty fiancée has been in town lately, busy with her trousseau and the furnisbing of a bunting box in Cheshire. They say the wedding presents are superb, and that Mist Sheridan bas no fewer than three engagement rings, half hoops ef sapphires, rubies, and diamends, all matched stones, while just thrown in for luck is a forth ring with one splendid emerald be- tween two big brilliants."

NEVER TRUST.

Never trust those who promise too much at a time. The patent medicines which pretend to cure "at once," in "a single dose," or in "ten minutes," convince us that they are unnatural in their operation, and that, therefore, either the promise made is a false one, or that the remedy is worse than the disease. It is not "at once" that a man becomes Consumptive; it is not "at a single meal" that you acquire Indigestion and Dyspepsia; it is not in "ten minutes' that your Liver becomes sluggish and out of order. No. These com- plaints and others which might be named are gradual in their hold to release you "at once," "in ten minutes,"or "at a single dose." As the disease, so its cure. Steady and certain is its approach. Steady and certain shoeld be its retreat. It is in this fact that the secret of the wonderful, the unparalleled success of Gwilym Evans' Quinine Bitters lies. It conforms to Nat ,re's laws, adapts itself to Nature's requirements. It does not pretend to miraculous instantaneous effect cures. If the disease is deep-rooted and of long sta.:ding, a sudden and forci- ble uprooting would do more harm than good. Gwilvm Evans Quinine Bitters loosen first one hold of the disease on the body,then another, and yet another, taking away one by one the clammy fingers of disease and re-placing them with the warm, glowing touch of health and new life,not only removing the disease effectually,but giving New Life with every dose taken. _ BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

ALL Tam' CLIMBING AFTER A PIGEON

- James Appleton,a young man, living with his parents at Winwick-road,Warrington,was brought up on remand before Alderman Wright (chairman), Dr. Gornall, and Councillor Henry Roberts, on Friday morning, charged with being found in the dwelling-house of Henry Bed- dard, 40, Winwick-road, with felonious intent.—Mr. Percy Davies represented the defendant.—Mrs.Beddard, wife of Henry Beddard.general dealer,4o,Winw:ok-road, stated that at about 2.30 on Tuesday afternoon she was in the house alone. She went upstairs and lay down on the bed in the front bedroom. All the doors in the house were bolted ,and the windows in the house were securely fastened. The back bedroom was a casement window and opened outwards and was broken a little. She heard the back-door latch lifted, and afterwards she heard a falling of glass in the back bodroom. She lay listening and then heard footsteps in the back bedroom and someone walking to the front bedroom. She shouted "Harry "but no one answered. There was a side-board near the door with a mirror in it and she saw the reflec- tion of a man. She got off the bed and caught the prisoner near the bedroom. She said to him" What brings you into any bedrooms ?" and he replied "I am only after a pigeon." He also said "I have taken noth- ing ; you will do nothing with me will you ?" She told him she would have him locked up and she then went downstairs and proceeded to Mr. Isherwood's shop for assistance. The prisoner went out by the back door which be unlocked. There were between £6O and £7O in the bedroom at the time. There was a crack in the wall and it was possible to see from one room to an- other.—ln cross-examination by Mr. Davies she ad mitted that the prisoner said "I heard you shou Harry, but I thought I would not run away as you might think I was after something."—lnspector Sninks gave evidence as to visiting the complainant's house and examining the back premises. He thought the prisoner would have fallen outwards and not inwards if he had been climbing after a pigeon. In cross-examination,witness said it the prisoner was hold- on by the inside of the window frame he might have fallen into the bedroom.—The Chief Constable said so far as the police records went they had nothing against the prisoner's character.—Detective Roper said he made en- quiries and searched for the prisoner on Tuesday night but was nnable to find him. He gave himself up on Wednesday morning at the Police Scation.—Mr. Davies for the detence explained the circumstances which led the prisoner to climb after his brother's pigeon, and the manner in which he fell into the bedroom. He submitted from the statements made to the complainant, the prisoner had no felonious intent.—The magistrates dis- missed the case, and the prisoner was then discharged. At the Borough Coutt on Monday, before the Mayor and other magistrates, Edward Lee, a stranger in the town who had been living for a few days at the Model Lodging-heuse, Rose and Crown-st., was charged on re- mand with having stolen a vest, a cardigan jacket, and a watch and chain, trom the Queen's Hotel yard on Saturday, the 12th inst.. value £2, the property of Richard Dennett, a person engaged in Mr. Connor's stables.—Detactive Monaghan stated that the prisoner had been knocking about the stables and the complainant employed him occasienally and had provided him with food. STOCKPORT A.—Rarely has an A team match been sa liberally patronised in Warrington as that which was played on the Wilderspool enclosure on Saturday, when there must have been 3,000 specta- tors in atteadance. Last season the Warrington second stringers, on their own soil, succeeded in beating the Stockport combination by one goal and two tries to a try,so that it was with some hopes of being able to maintain their unbroken record that the Warrington lads entered the field. The Warrington team was a strong one as will be seen from the following list of Warrington players :—Full back, J. Boscow ; three- quarter backs, Radford, Isherwood, Cromand O'Brien ; half backs, Fairhurst and Foden ; forwards, Thornton, Smith,Meacock, Sanders, Jenkins, Wilcock, Savage, and another. The kick-off was delayed for nearly half-an-hour, owing to the visitors making the journey—it is rumoured—without their football out- fit. After the preliminary exchanges, the home team soon showed a desire to score, and O'Brien was almost over in the first few minutes of the game due to a com- bined passing movement. A scrimmage ensued near the line but by short stages the visitors worked the ball to the 25 yard flag. The home team were here awarded a penalty kick by the referee—Mr. Jones, of Runcorn—for off-side play on the part of one of the visiting half-backs. Thornton was entrusted with the kick, and he placed a splendid goal. Encouraged by this success the home team played up vigorously, and the judicious kicking of Cross was noticeable. Eventually, Foden picked up the ball nicely after a scrimmage, and a smart passing movement ended in Wilcock crossing the line. The goal kick was a failure. This was not the whole :of the scoring before the whistle blew for half- time, for Cross after gaining possession, was to be seen careeriag through his opponents,and passing at the right moment to Radford that player scored near the corner. The kick at goal on this occasion was also a failure. After the restart, the visitors were unfortunate in losing the services of two of their players through injuries,and although they played up gamely, it was seen that they were a beaten team. Isherwood scored two tries,Wilcock a second one and O'Brien one. Of the four attempts to negotiate goals, only one was successful. The final arrived with the score : Warrington, 2 goals, 5 tries (23 points) ; Stockport nil.

LATCHFORD RANGERS Y. STOCKPORT RANGERS.

The Latchford Rangers engaged in a friendly match with Stockport Rangers on Saturday, this being the first occasion upon which the new ground •in Florist-street, Stockport, has been called into requisition. The teams were as follows: Latchford Rangers: Ashurst, back ; Buckley. Winstanley, Knight, and Jones, threequa.rter backs ; Robinson and Ellison, half-backs ; Donohue, Roberts, Lester, Fairhurst, Summers, Berry, and Brown, forwards. Rangers: Murdoch, back; Trevor, J. Lovery, Hussey, and J. Hilbert, threequarter backs ; L. Hazeldine and Holland, half-backs ; Faulkner. J. Richardson, Burrows, Johnson, A. Griffin, Flulmes, Yar- wooe, and Jackson forwards.—Haseldine won the toss, and decided to play up hill with the wind. Latchford started the ball,and the home lads took up the initiative, reaching their opponents 35. Here some keen give and take play ensued, and then from a rush Berrows dropped on the ball over the line, scoring an unmistake able try which Trevor converted in grand style. The visitors then had a look in, but before the interval ar- rived Trevor had hard lines with a penalty shot at goal from halfway. At the interval Stockport led by a gal to nothing. Faulkner re-started after a short respite, and the hornesters pressed. Grand passing by the visi- tors' backs, however, forced their opponents, and a try resulted,the point being converted. Both aides pressed in turn,and from a dribble, the Latchford lads added another try to their score, thus getting the lead. Re- sult : Latchford Rangers : 1 goal, 1 try (8 points) ; Stockoort Rangers, 1 goal (5 points).

RUNCORN v. WIGAN.

Played at Wigan before agate of about 4,000. Wigan made several changes in their team. The weather was very stormy, and a strong wind also blew from goal to goal. Punctual to the time, 3.30, the players turned out in the following order :—Runcorn: Houghton, back; Warder, Robinson, Butterworth and Richardson. three- quarters; Faulkner and Pendlebury, half backs; Faulk- ner, Farmer, Walker, Lightfoot, Hughes, Darlington, Reece and Gayter, forwards. Wigan: Winstantley, back; Flowers, Jago, Dixon and Timmony, three-quar- ter backs ; Foster and Halliwell, half backs ; Brown, Webster, Blackburn, Perkins, Walsh, Rigby, Benson, Catterall and Green forwards. Faulkuer won the toss, and elected to play with the wind in his favour. Mr. John Knowles, the president of the Wigan cluh,gave the initial kick, and Pendlebury made a poor return. The same player, however, a moment later kicked in touch, thus gaining some ground. Judicious kicking by the visitors got to the home end, where Robinson had a narrow shave of dropping a goal. Runcorn played finely. but Flowers neutralized well. Rain came clown smartly at this period and made the ball slippery, but still the Myrtle Greens kept operations located at the home end, where Dixon showed off his kicking abilities to advantage. Rain came down in torrents' which com- pletely saturated the players and spoiled the game. The men kept their places and strove hard, but many of the spectators vacated their positions for one of more shelter. The storm now abated , somewhat, but the ground was frightfully slushy. Pendlebury ran well, but Winstanley checked his career. Gradually Wigan made headway. Winstanley set the leather going again' and a free kick gained some ground. Fast play aelcentrefollowed Pendlebury,Robinson doing grand work, to which Winstanley replied well by finding touch. The rival backs tackled well, but Hnuglaton shone with the wind in his favour. Halliwell and Fos- , ter, the Wigan halves, were doing good work, the first- named kicking neatly into touch on several occasions. Butterworth next ran stronoy and looked like scoring, but his pass was mulled, and Flowers removed the dan- ger. Runcorn came again, but Dixon eventually sent , the ball back to the centre. Both sides worked hard,but forward rushes by the opposing players neutralized, the Runcorn men being very smart in the loose. They re- peatedly gained ground, but the sides were very well matched, taken all round. The visitors had the play frequently in the home half, but a fine defence was en- countered. Tunoney made his mark stuartly,and gained a little ground, but it was soon back again, and Pendle- bury was nearly over. A free kick for off-side gave Wigan relief. Half-time: Wigan nil: Runcorn nil. The Recond half was commenced in a very heavy thunderstorm. Faulkner started and Winstanley touched down. Timmoney got the ball into play,and for a short time the game was in the Runcorn half. Houghton, however, got it away to Flowers,fer the latter to send it back again. An effort by Dixon, Jago, and Halliwell was quickly checked, Butterworth forcing the latter to part. Faulkner followed with a free kick, which Win- stanley took sad easily returned. The forward play got keen, and there was some very strong tackling. On the ball getting loose the Cheshire halves gave Butterworth a chance to pass to Robinson, whom the referee brought back and the game was again contested in neutral terri- tory. The hostilities were conducted on the kick and rusts principle, Wigan taking the ball over the Runcorn line, but it went dead. This gave the Wigan players more courage, and they worked hard. A chance soon came to Butterworth. who gathered the ball in his own half, and raced down the field right over the Wigan line—a splendid individual effort. Hughes missed the goal kick. Soon after the start Richardson dribbled clear, and Butterworth picking up was nearly over once more, be being bald on the line. After some hard scrim- maging Halliwell started a movement to the other end, where play heated when the referee gave no side. Final score :—Runcorn, three points ; Wigan, nil.

At toe Warrington Borough Court on 'I ueeday, before Mr. E. J. Guest (chairman). Mr. R. Garnett, and Dr. Starkey Smith, a labourer named Ed ward Livesay pleaded guilty to an aggravated assault on his wife, Sarah Livesay, on the 221 d of July last. The prisoner's wife statpd that he was addicted to drink and when he was drunk he" took it out of her." On the date named she told him he must not take any more clothing out of the house for pledge, whereupon he struck her in the mouth, knocking one of her teeth out. Sae fell to the !bor.—The Chief Constable informed the bench that the prisoner had only just returned to town after an absence in Birmingham. He had never sent his wife any money for her support.—The prisoner said it was drink that had brought him to his present position, and if the magistrates would give him a chance this time he would promise them faithfully never to come there again.—The Chief Con- stable said the man was drunk when arrestod.—The prisoner admitted this and stated that when he took a drop of drink he was not right and lost his senses.—On the prisoner's who being asked whether she would live with him again, she stated that she preferred a separa- tion. She had not much faith in his promises.—Mr. Garnett : Your promises are something like those of the Sultan of Turkey—not much to be depended upon. —The magistrates committed prisoner to gaol for one month, expressing the hope that the prisoner would be teetotal after he came out of prison. He said he would be so.—A separation order was made,the prisoner to con- tribute 7s. per week to his wife's maintenance.

A LIVELY SCENE IN ORFORD•LLNEI

The Borough Bench was occupied for some consider- able time on Monday morning in hearing a case in which John Starkey, horse slaughterer. Irlam, and Ann Star- key and Martha Chadwick were charged with being drunk and disorderly in Orford-lane on the 10th inst.-, and Richard Chadwick, Irlam, charged with having been drunk while in charge of a horse and trap and as- saulting P.C. Monks on the 10th inst.—Mr. Percy Davies appeared for Richard Chadwick, and Mr. T. Granger for Mr. John Hughes, landlord of the Wheat Sheaf Inn.— P.C. Monks said that at about half-past nine an the night of the 10th inst. he saw all tLe defendants in a trap coming from the direction of Lrthgoes-lane. They drove up to the Wheat Sheaf Inn. The four defendants were drunk, and he requested them to go home and have no more drink. The defendant in charge of the horse told him "to go home and have a sleep." A man came out of the Wheat Sheaf with a tray, en which were glasses containing liquor but he objected to them having any more. Witness went to the defendant for the pur- pose of getting his name and address, but he refused to give it, and said he "lived anywhere." Mrs. Starkey fell out of the trap. Witness closed with Chadwiek, but with the assistance of those who surrounded them at the time Chadwick got away.—James Lomax said he 'saw • the trap being driven by Chadwick, and the people in it seemed to be in a drunken condition. Chadwick pulled up at the Wheat Sheaf, where a man went into the building. The police- man stepped across the road and objected to them hav- ing any liquor. He told them they were not in a fit state to drive a trap, and Chadwick came out and said he was not drunk. The officer said he was. Chadwick was in charge of the horse and he was in a staggering condition.—Henry Siddall said that on the date in eyes- tion hp was standing talking to Mr. Lema.x, when the trap containing the defendants drove up to the Wheat Sheaf. Chadwick was the soberest of the lot. He did not see anyone fall out of the tram—Harry Stirrup spoke to seeing a large crowd and one of the men lying down in the cart. Chadwick struck at the policeman, and the policeman warded it off and closed with him. , The defendants were" more or less drunk." He would net like to have trusted himself in the trap at the time. —John Hughes said he was landlord of the Wheat Sheaf. If the people had come in themselves he would have re- fused them. Two glasses of sherry and a small brandy were ordered by a man and he was taking them intothe parlour where there were customers. He did not know that the horse and cart were outside or the drinks would not have been served. If Chadwick was not drunk, he was very foolish. Mr.Daviee said he only represented one of the defendants Richard Chadwick, who was not a drunken person and who had never been accused of being drunk,and was an industrious and hard working man. What he wanted the Bench to understand was that Chadwick after half-past nine drove from Warringten to Cadishead and was not interrered with by an officer between Warrington and Cadishead. He said that he was not drunk at all. The accusition enraged him and made him do what he did. He never doubled his fist at him at all. He (Mr. Davies) only hoped that the Bench would make distinction as to the man in charge of the cart. There was no disorderly behaviour at all. Chadwick was very sorry that he did not give his name and address. He lost his temper, and gave his name as " Wigan,War- rington."—Matthew Hunt said he was in the neighbour- hood of the Wheat Sheaf at half-past nine on the evening of the 10th inst. Chadwick was !not drunk. Ho might have had some drink, but he was not drunk. He was in a fit state to drive a trap.—Mrs. Ann Starkey and Mrs. Martha Chadwick gave evidence as to their being sober, and as to the sobriety of Richard Chadwick.— Richard was fined 10s. 6d. and costs for the first offence and the charge of assault against the police was dis- missed. John Starkey for being drunk and disorderly was fined se. and costs, the charge against 'the women being dismissed.—Mr. Granger said Mr. hnghes asked for their worships to express an opinion that there was no blame attached to him.—Alderman Harrison: The publican has done nothing to incur the blame.

WARRINGTON v. STOCKPORT.

The Warrington team journeyed to Stockport on Saturday, and were once again defeated.
