THE STATE OF NEWFOUNDLAND.

I have, says a correspondent of the " Manchester Guardian," been allowed by the courtesy of the gentle- man to whom it is addressed to read a remarkably inter- esting letter wrtten by a correspondent resident in the United States who has recently passed some months on what is variously called the " French Shore "and the "Treaty Shore "of Newfoundland. The writer draws attention to the desirableness of a speedy settlement of the long-standing dispute with France being come to. With the historical diplomatic side of the dispute the writer does not concern himself, but of the lamentable practical evils that result from the existing state of things he is able to speak from close personal observa- tion. Newfoundland is by its geographical position "the key to America," and if only its resourc,s could be developed under normal conditions it should occupy a place at the head of Britain's colonial possessions instead of being near the foot. "It has, he says, " extraordin- ary mineral resources, unrivalled harbours, and in this part of the island [the letter is dated from Port-au- Port] a soil superior to most parts of the United States and a climate quite adapted for wheat growing, even to say nothing of the more hardy vegetables and grain." "The fisheries," he adds, certainly need no word as to their importance, except, iaerhaps, that every year the catch grows lighter because of the enormous drain, out of proportion to the breeding powers, of the cod and lobster." This falling off in the catch may be expected to become more and more marked unless some regula- tions are adopted to prevent fishing all the year round. At present there is no close season, and the rivalries of the Newfoundland and French fishermen make legisla- tion impossible. The " Treaty Shore" comprises by far the more habitable portion of the island, and every attempt to develop it is frustrated by the jealousy of the French. The French naval com- mander keeps a jealous eye on every new building which it is sought to erect, and, to the intense indigna- tion of the islanders, at once requests the British officer in command to have it removed. In this way every at- tempt to deveesp the resources of the country is nipped in the bud,and, as an American citizen, the writer thinks that it is " degrading " to Englishmen that they should thus have to carry out the behests of a French admiral in their own colony. " The fundamental grievance," the writer eontinues, " is that the citizens have to serve two masters ; that they are thereby kept in slavery, never seeing a pound from the year's beginning to its end,the merchants, who are the floating population and not subject to French supervision.taking all the fish and leaving nothing ; and, finally, that outside capital to de- velop the rich mines and agriculsural land cannot be in- troduced because the French allow no shipping and no legal:title to land occupied."

SHIP CANAL.

LAST WEEK'S TRAFFIC.

During the week ended September 18, 150 seagoing cargo veasels, with an aggregate net registered tonnage of 43,932 tons, arrived at and cleared from the port of Manchester. Of these 35 vessels,of 24,333, entered from or sailed for foreign ports, and 115 vessels, of 19,599 tons,were engaged in coastwise traffic. Among the ar- rivals were a steamer and two sailing vessels with tim- ber from Canada,three steansers with timber, pulp, &c., from the Baltic, five steamers with general cargo from the Contineht, three steamers with dints from France, a schooner with olive oil from C3ndia,a barque from Santa Elena with boneash and hornpiths, and one from Hayti with logwood. The first consignment of cotton to reach the Manchester docks this season arrived by the steamer Danish Prince, from ypt. The efforts to attract the Irish cattle traffic to the canal are meeting with success, and regular shipments are now being made from Dublin by the direct boats, Friday's steamer bringing 250 head of cattle, sheep, and pigs, besides a full general cargo. Ten vessels cleared outwards last week for Continental and Baltic ports,and there were also sailings to Bombay, Capetown (via Cardiff), Rio de Janeiro, New Orleans, Savannah, Progreso, and St. John, N.B.

LAST MONTH'S TRAFFIC.

Although the Ship Canal trftffic returns for August published on Saturday, do not quite equal the extra- ordinarily good record for July, they far surpass the figures for any previous month, and elearly prove that the July returns were the result of a permanent growth of trade and not merely an accidental accumulation of business within that period. It is also gratifying to note that the cargoes paying toll in August were, en the whole, of a more profitable character than in the pre- ceding month, the average toll per ton earned on sea- going traffic being—July is. 11 1-31.. August 2s. 0 1-51 Next month the cotton and grain imports will begin to tell upon the revenue of the waterway, and there is every reason to believe that the satisfactory average of the last two mouths will be at least maintained during the remainder of the year. TION SCHEME FOR MOTOR CARS.

The competition of Manchester with the shipping trade of Liverpool has now been sufficiently felt to Cause the shipowners and merchants of the latter city to become alive to the necessity of taking measures to retain their trade. A new scheme to this end 13 being promoted, and will shortly be put to practical test. It is intended to introduce motor cars, each one of which shall be capable of drawing three waggons carrying ten tons of produce. The waggons are to be loaded at the ship's side, and the special advantage of their use, it is contended, will be that go( ds can in this way be con- veyed without transfer to other vehicles direct to the warehouse, wherever this may be situated. The ordin- ary highways, of course,will be utilised for this purpose, and this without the laying of any rails or other mech- anical aids. It is expected by the promoters that by this means the cost of conveying merchandise to and from Manchester and other towns will be reduced to a point with which the Ship ..anal cannot compete, and the further hope is that the railway companies will be com- pelled in self-defence to lower their rates, which, again, will tell against the Ship Canal. It is suggested that the despatch of merchandise in the fashiou named should be reduced to a systeru,and that the night should be utilised as far as possible. Thus goods sent from Liverpool or Manchester at seven o'clock each evening would be in the , other city at their destination the first thing the follow- ing morning. It is believed that a fully loaded train of three waggons can be drawn at a uniform speed of six miles an hour.

Etttcrs to the e-Mor.

MR. KENYON ON THE MARRIAGE LAWS. Sir,—ln your issue of the 19th inst., Mr. Septimiss Kenyon airs some rather stale and misty views on tho marriage laws. True it is, that in the course of his somewhat confused letter he takes the credit of an original thinker, and expressos a longing desire that the world sheuld becon.e the happy possessor of his philo- sophical conclusions. Referring to the tragedy in Oliver- et., Mr. Kenyon remark; in his letter as follows: "Had the marriage laws been less binding and more just pro- bably that tragedy would not have occurred." Why, in the name f COMMOR sense, why? Does Mr. K-nyon read the papers? Has he never read of horrible tragedies occurring amongst people who were liv- ing together in defiance of the laws of marriage? Again is it not pissibis for couples who are mar- ried, and who find it impossible to live happily together under one roof, mutually to separate. and this without breaking the laws of marriage? It is a pity that such men as Mr. Kenyon d• not before rushing into print, try to make themselves acquainted with the salutary lessons contained in the history of humanity. Mr. Kenyon prates glibly about doing " ROMPthina to promote the beauty and the happiness of human life." Oh perverted ingenuity of man whither wit' thou lead us ! Mr. Kenyon's plea is for divorce a riamo He maintains that the laws of marriage are tro 4evore,harsh and cruel, and that these laws condemn to a l'fe of misery two souls who might find happiness if p-rmitted to 'lave their maniage annulled and to be san't-d with more congenial partners. Every law has its occasional inconveniences, and I admit that the law absolutely prohibiting divorce avinculo may sometimes appear rigorous and cruel. But its harshness Is mercy itself when compared with the frightfuLmiseries result- ing frem the toleration of divorso. Its inconvenience is infinitesimal when contrasted with the colossal evil, from which it saves society and the solid blessings it Secures to countless homes. Those exceptional ill- sorted marriages would becssmo mere rare if the public were convinced, once for all, that death alone can dis- solve the marriage bond. They would then use more circam9pection in the selection of a conjugal partner. ' To abolish this vital landmark of Christian civilisation would in due time reduce our populatiou to the level of barbarism. Mr. Kenyon would have been nearer the niqrb. if,instead of charging the marriage bend with the evils of the outrages he mentions, he h'Acl laid them at the door of the cursed habitr of drunkenness so preva- lent amongst us.—Yours, Scc.,

"SURSUM CORDA."

"THE LAWS OF MARRIAGE."

Sir,—Mr. Kenyon in his letter on the above subject publi, bed in your last issue, in his first sentence is at sea in blaming "the present laws of marriage" instead of the present liquor laws for " the tragedy" which oc- curred in Warrington on the 7th September. The whole of the evidence in relation to the tragedy is patent to any person of commonsense that drink,not the marriage Jaws, was at fault. Let Mr. Kenyon say why he shuns the drink facts of the case ? He cannot refute them, although he has ignored them. He talks about "that Tittle room in which lay the corpse of the maltreated woman being an epitome of the tragic side of life." But the brewery and alehouse are infinitely more blameable than "laws of marriage," and those who fatten also on the drink traffic than those who made and administer the laws of marriage. Our judges. chaplains,and magis- trates state that the driuk traffic is the chief cause of crime, &c. ,while our worthy Mayor at the late Brewster Sessions said part of our town is "a perfect hell"through drink. - I),aes Mr. Kenyen approve or ignore the evils arising from the drink business, because he uses his voting power with the drink party against Sunday Closing &e. ? How can it be that drink was the main cause of the poor woman's sad end, and Mr. Kenyon not admit the fact if he truly and purely aims to suppress one of the worst sources of crime, secial unhappiness, and divorces in our time and country ? Are not all who use their voting power &c., to put into ruling authority those allied with the drink traffic and who oppose such bills as the Sunday Closing of public-iteuees, and local vets, &e, in the very nature of the social and moral relation,' of society allied also in the terrible results of the drink evils? The social laws of life link the human family together like links in a chain, which cannot be strained, cr broken, without the whole feeling the effects thereof How divinely true it is, that, we are members one of another en divine principles, suffering or rejoicing to- gether. As a glass of pure water is nulluted by mingling a little foul with it,so have the whole of the tens of thou- sands of people in our borough, and thousands in other teems been pained in mind and heart by the tragedy in Oliver-street, "The eye caeinct say to the band, I have no need of thee, nor again the head to the feet, I have no need of you." Heartless criminals like" Cain cannot sat asid the immutable social law by feign ing ignorance about their brother and asking," Am I nay brother's keeper ?" "Be sure your sins will find you nut." So will the social law when violated always as in Cain's case be felt, and deplored. "My punish- ment is greater than I can Wear," he said. The brewers and distillers deadly business causes, says Dr. Norman Kerr, 120,000 to perish yearly,and the Rev. John Wesley says" All who sell drink in the common way to any that will buy, are poisoners generally. They murder His Majesty's subjects by whoseeale neither does their eye pity nor spare. They drive them to hell like sheep. And what is their gain ? Is it not the blood of those men ? Who then will envy their large estates and sumptuous palaces? A curse of God is in the midst of them ; the curse of God cleaves to the stones, the timber, the fur- niture of them !" &c. By abolishing the grocers' licenses , greatly reducing the number of drink shops, and obtaining Sunday clos- ing ; and, better still, personally abstaining from strong drink, aid securing prohibition for the nation we should thereby secure immeasurably more good—peace, unity, love and happiness for mankind than by simply alter- ing "the laws or marriage" so as to provide the easy separation to which Mr. Kenyon mostly refers. It is doubtful whether such an alteratien would not be pro- ductive of more social evil than of good. (1.) Would nrt many be more likely to marry from imPure motives than do new ? (2) Would net many obtain separation for mere trifles who are net now tempted to seek a separation ? (31 Would not more women and children tee lect helpless, and be a bigger burden to ratepayers? I think Mr. Kanyen has omitted the weightier matters in the laws of marriage. He has not a word of pity for the woman's slavish bend. "Serve him"—" obey him," he big slave Sze. Why should a woman have to make such omises any more than a man ? Too often the husband now through those slavish parts of the marriage laws demands from his wife un- just and unreasonable service and obedience. If Mr. Kenyon vraots that section of the law abolishing so do I, and I am with him if he wants the laws of marriage altering to make it legally as easy for wives to obtain a divorce when husbands are unfaithful and impure as it ie for the husbands now. I could bid him God Speed on such lines. But he cannot justly, or wisely ignore the evils that the liquor laws now inflict on scci pt-r generally. I trust he will no more play the part of a " Blind guide which strains at the gnat" of " the laces ef marriage," and swallows the camel of the present liquer laws.—Faithfully yours, HUMANITY. Warrington, September 24th, 196.

THE QUEEN'S REIGN.

The Queen has nose reigned longer than any of her 'predecessors on the English Throne. George 111. reigned 'ty-nine years three months and four days. Her T tjestv entered on the sixtieth year of her reign en the 20 June last, and on Wednesday, reckoning by days,and t tking account of leap years, ehe exceeded the period dirrine which George 111. occupied the Throne. Though Her Majesty has expressed a wish that all celebration of the auspicious event should be deferred until she has actually completed the sixtieth year of her reign, con- prarulations poured in upon her on Wednesday from all parts of the world. Among the first to offer their con- gratulations were her guests, the Czar and Czarina. The Queen has reigned for a greater number of years than any other European sovereign, and with the excep- tion of the King of Denenark,whe is about eleven months her senior, she is the oldest reiening sovereign. She has been Empress of India for twenty years, the addition to the Royal style having been made by proclamati on April 28, 1876. She has seen seventeen Presidents of the United States, ten Viceroys of Canada, fifteen Vice- roys of India,and France suecessively ruled by one King, one Emperor, and six Presidents of a republic. Her Majesty has outlived all those Members of the Mouse of -Peers who held seats ia it at her acceseion except Earl Nelson and the Earl of Darnles' (both of whom were -minors at that time) and all those Peer' who were then or had been previously members of the House of Coen- moue, except the Earl of Mansfield, the Earl f Mex- borough, and the Duke of Northumberland. She has seen five Archbishops of Canterbury, six Arehhishops of York, and four Bishops ef London, as well as eleven Lord Chancellors, ten Prime Ministers, and six speakers of the House cf Commons. She has out-lived all the members of her first Privy Council; and of her first fleuee of Commons only Mr. Gladstone, Mr. C. P. Villiers, Mr. J. Temple Loader, and possibly one or two others, now survive. Since her Majesty came to the throne sho haa seen every Episcopal t4ee vacated and re- filled twice at least, and most of them four or even five times, and the Judicial Bench recruited twice at the least from end to end ; and she has had four successive Esrls Marshal in the persons of four Dukes of Norfolk. She has outlived every member of the Jockey Club and every Master of Foxhounds that flourished in 1E37.

GOOD TEMPLARS.

The usual weekly session of the Hope of Earles- town lodge of Good Templars was held in the Co- operative Hall on Monday evening. Bro. W. Wil- son, C.T., presided over a large aaendance. Four candidates were initiated to membership and several proposed. Arrangements were made for the coming celebration of the introduction of the order into Earlestown, which will be the 24th anniver- sary. Sermons will be Preached on Sunday, and the following day Bre. J. S. Tarley, grand juvenile superintendent, will be the principal speaker. Other matters were delegated to the Business Com- mittee. The programme of the evening was ' Fruit festival," Bros. S. Barker and W. Wilson being res- ponsible. A large quantity of choice fruits was distributed. A collection was made for the forth- coming doll _competition at Bury.

In connection with the display at the nologii>al Gardens, Belle Vue, on Thursday, when over 200 of the veterans who took part in the Crimean am- paign were present and entertained to dinner, two local veterans who fought through the war were present, viz., Mr. John Baseldine, Earle-st., Earles- town, and Mr. C. Owen also of Earlestown. Both men are now working at the Viaduct Works. VULCAN WESLEYAN SUNDAY SCHOOL.

EARLIZSTOWN DISTRICT SCHOOL.

Reports of 11. M. Inspector which are of the usual high standard of excellence have just been received by the School correspondent, Dr. Handyside, and reftPct the greatest credit upon Mr. and Mrs. Brown and their respective staffs and on the management of the school. Mixed school :—"This is a thoroughly organised and disciplined school. The plans and methods of instruction are carefully thought out, and very effectively and skilfully employed. The staff of assistants renders most efficient aid. The highest possible grants have been earned in all the usual subjects as also in the two specific subjects taught, viz., domestic econorey and Algebra." We may add that two long additional class rooms (one specially fitted up for the teaching of cookery) are about to be opened to relieve those overcrowded schools. Drawing :—At the annual examination in this subject in May last, the highest award " ex- cellent," was made by the Science and Art depart- ment. Infant department :—Speaks eloquently for the energy, zeal and ability of the mistress to find the discipline and instruction so thoroughly sound and efficient, when due consideration is given to the serious difficulties she bat had to contend with during the greater part of the school year. The managers are about, perhaps have by this time begun, to build new class rooms for the mixed de- partment for the accommodation of the first standard taught here,and to remedy in some degree the defects." The highest grant has been earned. The number of children in attendance at the school is 900. It may not be out of place to mention that the four new teachers recently appointed in the mixed school, viz., Miss Adams (formerly a pupil teacher at this school) Miss Carter, Miss Reywood and Miss Napper were all placed in the first division at the last examination at their respective training colleges.

NOTES BY THE WAY &T R LEST OW N

BY AN OCCASIONAL CORRESPONDE NT.

_ The very inclement weather is playing sad havoc with the corps which are still ungarnered in this district. Some of them have been lying out for over three weeks, and there does not seem any probability of them beingigathered in for another three weeks, unless there is a great change in the atmospheric c3nditionr. Yet in the churches thanksgiving services are being held for the in-gathering of the harvest. To my mind, it seems hardly to be quite the thing to hold these thanks- giving services no matter what the state of the harvest may be,and leads me to think that the idea is chiefly to raise money, the thanksgiving being a minor consideration.

Prominence has been given to the fact that Lord Newton has promised a donation of .£l,OOO to the extension fund of St. John's Church. It is certainly correct that his I.ordship has promised to contri- bute this sum, but the committee cannot reckon upon it as an assured sum just yet. It appears it is h dged round with certain conditions,the principal being that should the extension cost say £7,ooo,and the committee have £6,000 towards it in hand,Lord Newton will give his £l,OOO to make up the amount required.

Scarlet fever is still very prevalent in this dis- trict, the portion chiefly affected being the Corn- mpn and Newton. In all cases where isolation cannot be secured, the patients have been removed to the infectious diseases hospital at Warrington. It will be pus seen that every effort is being made to prevent the spzead of the disease, and although the cases are amongst school children they are not so numerous as to be described as epidemical and it has not so far been found necessary to interfere with the schools.

Talking about schools reminds me that the edu- cation question is becoming a rather serious one in Earlestown in the voluntary schools. We have So far been able to keep out the School Board, but my impression is that it is looming right ahead,and that its introduction may not be such a great way off. During the next week or two a house to house can- vass is to be made on behalf of the District Council School Attendance Committee, for the purpose of REcertaining how many children there are of school age. The maximum age has been fixed at 15, so that particulars will have to be given of all children up to that :aze. Householders therefore need not be alarmed when the canvasser calls for informa- tion.

THE EXAMINER' SATURDAY_ SEPTEMBER 26, 196.

ADJOURNED LICENSING SESSIONS AT WA RRIN GTON.

Thursday was the day fixed for the holding of the adjourned licensing sessions at Warrington,but, in striking contrast with the sessions held on pre- vious occasions, there was a difficulty in obtaining sufficient magistrates to preside on the Bench. In order te hear an application which was to be made it was necessary to have the presence of three mem- bers of the Licensing Committee,but at ten o'clock only two had put in an appearance,viz :—Mr. John Fairclough, and Mr. E. J. 6uest. Telephonic mes- sages were sent to the business establishments of the other members of the committee but it MIA found that the majority were out of town. As a last resource a cab was despatched for Mr. J. W. Hayward, and at 10.30 that gentlemnn arrived and thus prevented a further adjournment of the ses- sions. The application to be heard was that made by Mr. Benjamin Kenyon of the "Green Man" beerhouse, Bank Quay, for a wins license to sell on the premises. The applicant appeared in person, and said ha had made the ap- plication for three successive years. "..5n the first occasion there was no objection what- ever to it, and he did not know of any opposition this time. He was repeatedly asked for wine by customers and a wine license was needed very much.—The Chief Constable said he did not think that if the wihe license were granted it would do much harm or much good. The " Green Man" was a well-conducted bserhouse and one of the best in the town. There was a fully-licensed house called the Bowling Green 140 yards away, also the Ship Inn, 198 yards away, and the Railway Hotel 398 yards away. The Bowling Green and the Ship Inn were in Liverpool-road, and the Railway Hotel in Milner-street. He had visited the house and he found it was very well kept aid well conducted. It was not a very large house. consisting of two par- lours, a taproom and a bar. There were no drinking rooms upstairs. Some people did not drink beer and might want a glass of wine. It might be a con- venience to them if they could be supplied with it at this hottse.—The application was refused.

IRISH NATIONAL LEAGUE OF GREAT BRITAIN.-WARRINGTON BRANCH.

ANNIVERSARY OF ROBERr EMNIET.

A "meeting tinder the auspices of the above organisa- tion was held in the Druids' Hall on Sunday,to celebrate the anniversary of Robert Emmet. The chair was takan at 3 p.a.. iv the ?resident, Mr. J. C. Dalton, who was supported by Mr. Scully, J.P., chairman of the Gorton Urban Beard ; Professor O'Brien. of Manchester ; Mr. Kilduff, Mr. Roddy, Mr. P. Lane, Mr. Malone, Mr. Lyons, Mr. Thomas Li!gallon, hoa. treasurer, and others. The hall was crowd's!, and the meeting was of an enthusias- tic character. .

hd CIirAIRM. AN in his opening speech remarked that they were met that day to commemorate the anniver- sary of a man wit° was ne assassin. He was a true hero who hesitated not to dare the enemy in the open in hottest strife. The Irish nature was averse to the policy of assassination and outrage. Tits English Govern- ment were the real promoters of all the conspiracies of a base nature that had ever existed in connection with the Irish difficulty. Let England give to Ireland the measure of self-Gevern- mint she asked for, and such a thing as an Irish dyna- mitard would be a thing of the past. They the people who formed the Irish party, believed that Ireland could gain her rights without any law of God or of man being violated. In order to fully appreciate the lessons taught them by the life and death of Robert Emmet, they should become conversant with the fearful effects of the penal laws under which the people suffered. Some of them would doubtless like to heir from him an account of the deliberations of the great convention recently held in Dublin. He would briefly annouece to them the message of the eonvention to the Irish people. It was twofold in its nature. First the message commanded them to unite, to obey, and to assist the true leaders of the people of Ireland to put down insubordination, to turn out the traitors, and to create a real party, which would be loyal and true, and constant in its demand for Irish freedom. The second portion of the message was that they must maintain an independent party pledged to uphold majority rule. The Chairman then intro- duced

Mr. SCULLY, Justice of the Peace for the city of Man- chester, who in a forcible and effective speech gave a graphic description of the condition of:lreland at the titne•of Robert Emmet. He was followed by Professor O'Brien who delivered a most eloquent panegyric on Robert Emmet. Mr. O'Brien concluded his learned dis- course by exhorting the Irish people of Warrington to rally round the green flag raised in their midst. Mr. O'Brien was frequently interrupted by the loud applause w icla greeted his eloquent and burning words. The following resolution was proposed by Mr. Lyons and seconded by Mr. Killduff :—‘• Resolved, that this meeting records its appreciation of the noble spirit ex- hibited by Robert Emmet, by his pure and devoted life, andihis most glorious death ; given freely in the cause of freedom. And we desire that his example will be the means of emulation among our young Irishmen on be- half of their country ; likewise we desire to place on record, our disgust at the cowardly conduct of the Con- servative Government now in power,for not taking long ago the initiative in attempting to put a stop to the horrible atrocities perpetrated by the unspeakable 'I urk." The resolution was carried with acclamation. Subse- quently Mr. Malone, Mr. Lane,and Mr. Roddy addressed the meetirw, which af tor the usual votes of thanks had been adopted and responded to, was closed by the sing- ing of "God save Ireland "by the whole audience.

BOWLING.

LANCASHIRE AND CHBSBIRE ASSOCIATION. FINAL TIR.—VICTORY OF WARRINGTON SOUTH. WARRINGTON SOUTHv. Imen.—These teams played off their tie in the final for the Association prize on the green of the Radcliffe Club at Withins on Saturday. It will be remembered that both teams were defeated in the semi- final of the senior competition last month, and under the conditions which govern the contest they were entitled to compete for the handsome prizes offered by the Assoc- iation for clubs who have been se successful in reaching so far a stage. There was a fair attendance, and the first pair to try conclusions were J. Gleave and T. Collier. The former took a lead at the onset, and though Collier managed to" peel" at the tenth end, the Warringtonian never allowed his opponent another opportuaity after this, and at the close Gleave had won handsomely by seven paints. T. Hackett played a consistent good game and though for the major point of his match with R. Lathem he was in arrear, he played up pluckily at the close, and excellent bowling from the 23rd ond lauded him a winner by five points. E. Shaw mat W. Cocker, and the Leigh player early gained a substantial lead allowing the Warringtonian little opportunity, and at the close Cocker gained the game by a majority of five points. It was a rare game between T. Locker and 11. Forshaw,and the issue hung in the balance nearly up to the close. The players repeatedly peeled,but at 18 each Forshavr secured the jack, and ultimately ran out winner by three shots. It was refreshing to see J.Shaw play quite up to his old form, and though J. H. Cocker gave trouble at the onset, the Warringtonian eventually bad a Icing run if success, and won his game in hand- some style by eight points. W. Bolton proved himself a consistent plat or, and his victory over R. Radcliff was well merited. B. Hall succumbed to W. Houghton, and the veteran South player was behind almost throughout his game, losing at the close by nine points. P. Wells gained a substantial victory over S. Hayes by eight points, and from the tenth end the issue was never in doubt. It was left te D. Wilcox, the South skipper to gain the most decisive victory of the day. From the time the first wood was delivered, Wilcox never allowed his opponent isuch chenee,and from the fourteenth end Isherwood failed to score a chalk,the Warringtonian)win- Ding by a dozen points. The game between F. Berry and J. Southern was an exciting affair throughout, and it was not until this game had run into thirty ends that Southern gained the victory for his side by a single point. W. Shaw failed to maintain his reputation, and his decisive defeat at the hands of W. Collier by eleven points was one of the surprises of the day. The last game of the afternoon saw another Warringtonian being subjected to defeat, R. Fairbrother only making 17 points against 'l'. Smith's 21. It will be seen from the table below that of the dazen games played each side claimed a like number of victories,but the Warring- tonians accomplished theirs in so decisive a style as to give them the distinction of winning by 13 points. WARRINGTON AND THE ARM':;NIA.N ATROCITIES.

REFERENCES AT CAIRO-STREET CHAPEL:

The pulpit at the above place of worship on Sunday evening was occupied by the Rev. W. H. Drnmmond,tise pastor,who spoke upon the crisis in Constantinople and the Armenian massacres. He remarked that the call of God caine not only to individual men but also to nations of men,and they were at the present moment standing at a period of their history in which a great divine call had come to them,a call from a field red with slaughter and rapine and lust. They in that chapel had not forgotten the address of Professor Thoumaian delivered it') that pulpit nearly a year ago oa the Armenian massacres. Since then, for a few short months, they seemed to have heard less of these ghastly horrors, but those of them who had gene at all closely into the question were hardly surprised that the respite was a brief one, and that now these horrors had come nearer home ; no longer in the mountain fastneases of Armenia, but actually in the streets of Constantinople. He was not that night going to harrow their feelings with any description of scenes which they already knew toe well. That surely was quite need- less. Where was there a man who had read with ordin- ary care his daily paper during the last few weeks whose flesh had not crept, whose heart had not quailed, as he read of things which entered like iron into the blood, and which would haunt them as dreams of horror to their dying day ? And ia face of these things, things so terrible that language itself almost failed to tell Of them, they found the great Christian powers of Europe doing nothing, debating amongst themselves how they might escape from doing something, tolerating this hide- ous crime in their midst rather than rue any national risk. He knew of nothing in recent European history which was a mere ghastly commentary upon the hollow- ness of their Christianity. But there were signs that feelings his England were at last being stirred by the spectacle of things somewhat nearer home, and they wore profoundly thankful that at last the common Chistianity of their country had found its deep voiee up- lifting itself everywhere in protest and indignation. And now they might ask him what could they do? They were all horrified at these things. They could to a certain ex- tent measure the terrible menace that they were to their western civilization. "But what can we do?" they asked. It seemed but a small thing to speak burning words and hold deeply moved public: meetings, but if that was all they could do then let them ia God's name do that, and let them not be misled in thinking that even these things did not mean much. In the first place they could be in deadly earnest about this matter. The time had gone by for talking ;shoat it in easy tones, and , wondering whether after all things were quite as bad as they were painted ; that they had got troubles enough at home, and why, therefore should they trouble them- selves about things a thousand miles away? The time had gene by for all that, and every true English heart must be in dewnright earnest on this matter. If they said that they did not know much about it, then he said that it was their duty to know about it. There were some things of whisk ignoranee amounted almost to a crime, and when their fellow Christians, whom time and again they had promised to protect, were being exterminated with every barbarity, then they as Englishmen were bound to know about it and to be earnest in the way ef approaching the subject. In the second place they could recognize the power of the people at a time lilts this. England was a democracy. That meent that ultimately government and great deci- sions of national duty were in the hands of the people. It was true that they had to trust a few gifted men to conduct delicate negotiations, to frame their laws, to carry on the machinery of political government,but they were powerless unless they felt the force of the people behind them, and it was from the people and from them alone that the moral force and the conscience of political action came. And so it came to this that in all matters of grave national duty, when they were in danger of neglecting to do what God called them to do, it was for the people to speak and to speak with no unz:ertain sound and to say it, not without reflection, that the time at which they had arrived now in this perplexing Eastern question was one in which the people must take the matter into their own hands, and speak with a deci- sive voice. In the third place they Gould lay hold of the great Bible words, as their daily salvation at a: crisis of this kind—that "righteousness exalteth a nation," and put al! selfish considerations behind their back. There were many men who were telling them to be prudent,to be cautious, to move slowly in the matter. There were men who were appealing to the instincts of self-preserva- tion, and there were others who were working upon the selfishness of their commercial instincts, and these men were doing all that they could to prevent any intervention on the part of this country. New let him put an extreme case. Suppose any action they might take to put an end to these indescribable horrors, to this iniquitous system of government in the Turkish empire ; suppose any steps they felt bound to take should mean loss of national prestige in the eyes et semi of the , ether countries of Europe ; eunpose it should mean some actual curtailment of their commerce ; suppose it should mean some actual suffering to some large number of their people, was that any reason why they should not do that which was right? Our Lord had said that the man who would save his life should lose it, and he was sure He also would have said that the nation that would save its life at the cost of national duty should lose it ; for after all what was it that made a nation great, what was It that made them proud of being Euglisit- men ? Not surely the gigantio erea of their colonies,not the fact that they were sufficiently powerful for other nations to be jealous of them, but it was the solid and enduring qualities of the English character which had built up:reetional4reatnesB. And they could only keep these qualities and only preserve their greatness in proportion as they did their duty. They were great in so far as they had the national conscience and national opportunities of right doing, and if they refuse to do their duty because they were afraid that it might mean some suffering for themselves, siaould they have any reason ever afterwards to be proud of the English name? And so he asked them as members of the Church of Christ, to listen to the call of God which was coming to them in the midst of their thanksgiving—their happiness and their tranquility—coming to them from the harvest fields red with blood, which were waiting not for the happy songs of the gleaners but for the dark wings of the angel of judgment. SERMON BY THE REV. J. YONGE.

The Rev. John Yonge spoke at Wycliffe Church upon the subject of the Armenian atrocities on Sunday even- ing,taking for his text the words " Deliver the poor and needy ; rid them out of the hand of the wicked." (Psalm lxxxii-4). The preacher at the outset remarked that the psalm appeared to Abe intended as a solemn charge to those who held office as judges and rulers of the people, and it seemed that so long as the Christian Powers of Europe maintained friendly relations with the iniquitous Sultan of Tuekey they deserved the rebuke of the second verse—" How long will ye judge unjustly, and accept the persons of the wicked ?" Having re- ferred to the apathy which had previously prevailed upon this subject,Mr. Yonge went on to say that now, however, the state of opinion had changed, and every day was showing that there was throughout the kingdom a tremendous uprising of inter- est, sympathy, and indignation upon this most serious matter. The nation was taking fire ; it was awakening to the consciousness of a national and Continental crisis that might lead to speedy events ef the largest significance. There could, therefore, be no longer any doubt as to tho propriety and even necessity for plain speech from Christian pulpits as to what was becoming the duty of Christian nations, and of their own nation in particular. The Rev. John Yonge then proceeedea to graphically describe the position of affairs, pointed out some of the horrors of the massacres, and read an extract from the report of an eye witness who had been successful in escaping to London, to show that the barbarities had not been exaggerated in the public press. These were, Mr. Yonge said, only random ex- tracts from the harrowing and ghastly details of those awtal days. The Embassies of the European Powers were for the time horrified, and they did not hesitate to —what? Protest ! They even told the Sultan that he was eadangering his dynasty by these deeds of blood. Also they went sofar as to refuse to illuminate,as was custom ary, on the anniversary of the Sultan's accession, which day coincided with the massacres. The preacher added that even up to the present the authorities of the Porte were sending the Armenian dwellers in the city by a series of ship-loads avowedly back to Armenia. There was, however, every reason to believe that they never reached Armenia. Canon Rawnsiley, of Carlisle, preach- ing a fortnight ago in the Parish Church of Crosthwaite, reeds the following awful statement which was said to be an ascertained tact :—" Same little time since a diver went down to his work in the Bosphorus, and found himself suddenly in the presence of what seemed to be an army of men standing upright in the swaying tide. There they stood with lead weights at their feet and heavy stones around their ankles—an army of dead men. Ile counted 40 from whore he stood. That was but a detachment of hundreds of Armenians who had been taken from the city and carted into the deeps. One night recently 300 men were spirited aboard an old ves- sel, and that vessel put off at deau of night into mid-tide and had never more been seen." Such were some of the horrors transacted even in Constantinople itself, under the very windows of the Embassies of Russia, Austria, Germany, Italy. France and England. And yet all that these six Powers could agree to do was to protest, and warn, and consult, and diplematize. Than what was the plain duty of the six Christian Powers in the face of that diabolical devastation of a Christian people? Three words defined that duty, their duty was to stop it. What any half olczeu passers-by would do if they saw a fiendish brute committing any such atrocities as those upon a defenceless man or woman,thesesix powers ought to dc) with that crowned malefactor and murderer. They ought to stop him at once and for ever. Well, why did they not do it? Remember, they were Chris- tian Powers, and it was a Clirician people that was being done to the death. Therefore, every religious sympathy should be impelling them to act. Every hum- ane motive should constrain them. Why did they not interfere? The only true answer to the question was a scandal to their commea Christianity, and a disgrace to humanity itself. They did not interfere ; they allowed the Imperial murderer to go on in his hellish work—they

suffered hundreds and thousands of innocent and helpless Armenians to be dishonoured, ruined, beggared, tortured, drowned, and slain, because they feared any active interference would imperil their own particular interests. National selfishness and mutual jealousy in every case were making the European Powers inactive. They were afraid of one another, afraid that if the Turkish empire became broken up— which it must be sooner or later—they could not all get out of it what they wanted. They foresaw a quarrel, but could not foresee either its extent or its issues, and so they did nothing. And they were bent on hindering one another from doing anything. Rather than imperil their interests they would not "deliver the poor and needs', nor would they rid them out of the hand cf the wicked." Time that night forbade him dealing in detail with that mutual selfishness of the six Powers. But they were bound to say this, that as Christian Powers they stood convicted before the bar of Christianity of infinite and unpardonable selfishness. And more, as they who would net prevent sin when it was in their power to do it be- came partakers in it, so these rowers became morally responsible, along with the Sultan of Turkey, for the whole of that Armenian slaughter. He did net see how believers in national righteousness, and believers in Divine rule and righteousness, could hold any other opinion. The whole of European Christendom at any rate was implicated in that awful guilt, and so long as they continued inactive their responsibility must go on. Their duty was to stop that sin, and if they did not stop it the sin became their own, and that was how the case stood as he lavelit, in the light of truth. Seeing then that the six eowers hesitated to act, what became the duty of the sem to which they belonged? That was the question which after all they had to face. It would seem from the events of the past two or three weeks that the British people were ROW rapidly coming to believe that they had a peculiar duty and responsibility in that Armenian crisis. The apathy of a year ago was gone. The difference of opinion as to national reeponsibilisy was also largely disappearing. The feeling was intensifying that they could not much longer endure the spectacle provided for them by the Sultan of Turkey. The cry of perishing Armenia was becoming tee piercing, too agonizing to be longer disre- garded. Their Christianity was thrilling with sympathy. Their most humane sentiments were 'stirred, and they were calling to mind that they had treaty engagements, which involved them in peculiar obligations. What were these? In 1878 there was made an Anglo-Turkish con- vention by which England engaged to keep Russia out of Armenia on condition that Cyprus was ceded to her by Turkey, and that the Sultan should introduce reforms for the better government and safe pro- tection of his Christian subjects. There was the special responsibility entailed upon them of seeing that the Turkish reforms should be carried out. But also by the Berlin treaty of the same year religious liberty was to be granted by the 'Suiten to all hi 3 Chris- tian subjects, and by the 61st article of that treaty the Porte undertook, without further delay, to provide for the better condition of Armenia, and to guarantee its security against Circaesians and Kurds. None of these pledges had ever been redeemed. rho Berlin treaty had been treated as a dead letter ; and the Anglo-Turkish convention also, that was, by Turkey. Well, if the six Powers would not combine to enforce the Berlin treaty, then let them combine to compass the same end in some better way. But if they would not move at all, then it became the peremptory duty of this country to discharge its own responsibility. What he thonght they needed to feel was that they had then come to the point when that matter rite far beyond all party politics. It could then only be dealt with on the high grounds of national Christian duty. Two lines of action, it seemed to him, lay before them as a nation. The first was this : a deliberate and determined endeavour to once more bring about a settlement of the concert of Europe. Their Government should make it perfectly clear to all the Cabinets of Europe that they had " no axe of their own " to grind in that matter; that they were not the utterly selfish nation they were willing to think them, and for which unhappy opinion they might be somewhat themselves to blame, but that what they did Peek was the ending of a Power at Con- stantinople which had been a cause of continual unrest to Europe. and hopelees misery to its own subjects, and the establishment of purity and righteousness in its stead. Let their Government de more than use lan- guage—let it offer distinct and definite pledges of their sincerity. Let it declare that the work should be done by any one, or any two,of the Powers. Only let it make it perfectly plain that if those proposals were declined England would do her duty, if need be, single- handed and alone. They might hope indeed that some happy effect uponithat matter might come of the visit of the Czar to their Queen this week. They should all join in the earnept prayer that no fell deed of diabolic hate might be attempted against that young monarch as he came to visit his venerable relative. But they should be equally earnest in desiring that the aged Queen and youthful Czar might so confer at that critical affair of international state that good might follow. But if not then assuredly " we must pause no longer but go for- ward trusting in God." Mr. Norton, of London, said the ether day that he spoke but the thought of all when he said that he for one was ready to dare all and sacrifice all to end that unspeakable iniquity in Europe. Even if England were left in isolation and should perish, better so than to bear the sting of shame, that they pledged themselves to secure a perishing people, removed them from the protection of a power which would in a way have secured them, and then hung back ' for fear of consespences. They were men of Peace, they served the Prince of Peace,hut the Prince of Peace was also the God of Battles, and his servants for- bidden to fight in self-defence et. in personal vengance I were by all their vows called to put on the helmet of 1 salvation and gird themselves with the sword of fied,to succour the oppressed and to bind up the bleeding sore of a natien. In those sentiments he agreed. They I might appear extreme, but they were not extravagant. They had been driven to extremes, and in such a case it became them to be strong and of good courage. " Net once or twice in our rough island story, the path of duty is the way to glory." It was, however, by no I means certein that the duty would prove to be so perilous as it looked. It did not appear to be at • all inevitable that even the single- handed action or Great Britain would provoke a European war. Neither was it certain that if they began alone they would be left alone. The point how- ever was that—be the peril what it may—even should they be left to face the fury of the nations, they should be engaged in a conflict for the right. They would be engaged once more in a conflict where the country had won many laurels, the struggle for freedom, and in such a strife greater was He that was on their side than all they that could be against them. Now having those views of the present crisis and the duty it involved, it became them to join in the movement now spreading to express the national feeling and to support the Govern- ment of the Queen. They should do SO with the greater earnestness because so much peril to huntanity seemed ins olved in the delay. Said one of the best informed correspondents of the Press—" There is a pause in the work of maneacre, but Constantinople is a hell, and the next massacre will not be confined to Armenians, nor will it be unresented. If the Powers do nothing, we shall see all hell let loose throughout the Empire." Let them then speak out calmly but decidedly, assuring the Foreign Minister that they would uphold him in a firm, manly and Christian procedure of espousing the cause of the oppressed, by challenging the co-operation of Europe for their help, but failing that let him commit England to a task which she had achieved before, and by the help of God she could achieve again. Ever might their nation be found both willing and able to" deliver the poor and needy, and rid them out of the hand of the wicked,"

A RESOLUTION PASSED.

Mr. ZIBX ARMITASH said with a deep sense of respon- sibility be moved the following resolution :—" Resolved (1) That this congregation, assembled in Wycliffe Con-gregational Church, Warrington,.oe Sunday eveninte September 20th,1896, having cens.idered the present ter- rible events affecting the Artm.inian. people, desires to express its deep concern and disapeintment at the con- tinued ieaction of the Powers of Europe, and earnestly impleres her Majesty the Queen to appeal to the Czar of Russia, during his Majesty's visit to Balmoral, in the name of Christianitylaud humanity, begging him to co- operate with Great Britain in rescuing the surviving Armenians from the terri fie outrages and slaughter by which so many thousands of their countrymen have beers visited at the hands of the Sultan. (2) That this assembly assures their Majesties' Governments of hearty support in speedily adopting, if possible, in alliance with other Powers,effec:ive measures to stay the hands of the Sultan from further outrage and barbarity, and to bring to an end a reign and Government that are a scandal to humanity and a curse to the world. (3) Finally, that this congregation earnestly prays that the Government may be strong and of good courage, and be Divinely directed in all its councils and decisions." Councillor HENRY ROBERTS, J.P., seconded the resolu- tion. Re said that when he received Mr. Yonge's re- quest to second that resolution he felt himself inade- quate to do justice to the importance of it. But the sermon to which they had listened had tio fully and com- pletely stated the whele case an I aeticipated anything he might say that it was quite sufficient to formally second the resolution. He might add that lie did net think it was impossible for any of th, in who were out- side the diplomatic circle to thoroughly understand the terrible crisis which faced the Ministers of her Majesty, and while tksere might be reasons to explain the splendid isolation in which we stood to-day still It was not a time for criticism, but a time when every man and woman who desired the peace of the Armenians should do all that lay in their power to support Her Majesty's advisers in what they might con- sider necessary or desirable to bring to an end the pre- sent condition of thiugs in Turkey. They all hoped that the visit of the Czar of Russia to this country might bring with it a peaceful, and let them hope a speedy ter- mination of these horrors. If it were thought neces- sary, surely the Queen would endeavour to use her influ- ence so that our country should act and co-operate with Russia in this matter. But whether that were dame or not, we had in years gone by inaple ourselves responsible to these poor people of Armenia by the authority under which:welkept them out of the hands of a friendly power, to protect their lives and property, whatever might be the consequences and the loss to ourselves. It was our

duty to let it be understood that these things would not be tolerated by this country any longer.—Tbe congrega- tion signified their approval of the resolution by standing up in a body.

RESOLUTION PASSED BY THE WYCLIFFE P.S.A. SOCIETY.

The weekly meeting of the Wycliffe P.S.A. Society was held on Sunday afternoon in Wycliffe Church. Mr. William Roberts presided, and in referring to the crisis in Turkey said he was glad that the hearts of the people had been stirred in denouncing the abominable murders which had been carried on in Constantinople. They did not speak in any sense as politicians but he hoped they spoke as Christian men and women and as aChristian na- tion. It was not possible for him to exaggerate the enor- mities which had been perpetrated, and he was sure he was speaking the sentiments of everyone present when he said that they were disgusted at the time which had been allowed to elapse without anything of a tangible nature being done to put a stop to the abominable wickedness of the Turk. It seemed a matter that could not be ex- plained away that the large powers of Europe should almost stand aside and watch the evil going on and yet be powerless to stop it. he thanked God that the coun- try had at last awakened to the necessity for prompt and immediate action. It must be gratifying to notice that in that matter the country had awakened to a sense of its responsibility without any leaders. (Hear, hear). The indignation had come as it always had come from the masses of the people and be believed the feeling of the country at the present moment was that they must stand no further nonsence in regard to these atrocities. During the past two years it was estimated that no fewer than 200,000 people had been massacred at the instigation of the Sultan of Turkey—or four times the population of Warrington massacred. If war was necessary to put a stop to this wickedness, then let there be war, and God defend the right. (Applause.) The address for the afternoon was delivered by Mr. Lennox, of Manchester, and the musical portion of the service was made additionally attractive by the singing of a solo by Mr. Fieldhouse. It was announced by the Rev. John Yenge that the membership now totalled 1021. Referring to the Armenian question, the rev. gentleman said it was suggested the previous Sunday that a petition should be sent from the members of that P.S.A. Society, but he had noticed that several P.S.A. societies in the country had passed a resolu- tion, and he thought that course would be the better one to take. He would therefore ask two members to propose a resolution to be sent to Lord Salisbury and Lord Rosebery.—Mr. Haley then moved a resolution, desiring to join in the widely spreading expression of disgust at the events in Turkey and assuring the Government that any measures taken either in conjunction with other powers or not for put- ting an end to the reign of terror in Turkey would meet with their unanimous support.—Mr. Hickman in second- ing the resolution said he wondered that they had been silent so long on this very grave and important question. The resolution was a protest against the inhuman out- rages and bloed-curdling massacres which had been tak- ing place amongst their brethren in Armenia, and he had pleasure in seconding it.—The resolution was unani- mously passed, each atember of the congregation stand- ing up. THE CZA.R'S VISIT TO THE QUEEN. The Czar and Czarina arrived at LAth on Tuesdai morning, and in the evening their Majesties reached Bal; moral, where they are the guests of the Queen. A' Leith, Dundee, and Aberdeen there was some cereinonY in connecticn with the Czar's journey, addresces of wel' come being presented by the civic authorities. An ad" dress from the Corporation of Ediniiurgh expressed e hope that the Russian and British peoples, knit together, by mutual sympathy, would not merely enjoy moral an" material prosperity within their borders, but that: their united influence would be potent in the interest of peace and in advancing the cause of humanity and civilier tion throughout the world. The Dundee address sala the signatories hoped the Czar and Czarina would 101 l be spared to promote and ennerve the true Peace 0' nations and extend the civilisation of the world, whil! the Aberdr,en address touched upon the relations 0; peace and friendship between England and Russia, eau expressed a desire that the Czar would be spared to dr velop the resources of his vast empire. His Majestyl reply in each case was limited to an expression of mania The day was wet, but crowds assembled wherever there was an opportunity of seeing the Czar, and his receptioe was a very cordial one. Although a good deal of rain fell at Balmoral on Wed' nesday, the Czar of Russia was not kept wholly witbio doors. Accompanied by the Prince of Wales and the Mike of Connaught, His Majesty went during 't he after' noon to Abergeldie Forest for a deer drive, and a Arie stag is reported to have fallen to his gun. • A youth of seventeen named Joseph Rows died oe Saturday from injuries supposed to have been sustained while playing football on the previous evening at New' ton Cap, Bishop Auckland.
